# PREFACE.

*...in a proper spirit, in this first and most important article* <note>In this context, an "article of religion" refers to a specific point of religious doctrine or practice.</note> *of religion; wherein the good, even of Society and* *Government, is so essentially concerned.*

*We need not wonder, at the absolute depravation of* *manners, so very dangerous to public security; now let* *loose, among the vulgar <note>In the 18th century, "vulgar" referred to the common people or the general public, rather than meaning "crude" or "gross."</note> and I wish, the example was* *not wanting in those of superior class; to prevent the* *alarming danger of immorality, with open flood-gates* *rushing in upon us: owing to the egregious profanation.*

*The whole course of learning is requisite, to the study* *of Divinity, <note>Divinity refers to the study of theology and religious matters.</note> if we would exhibit its true grandeur, and* *genuine beauty. such has been my endeavor. for all* *studys <note>An older spelling of "studies."</note> should be directed to it; to the general and most* *effectual good of mankind: agreeable to the purpose of* *divine providence. he designed the world, for a para-* *dise; and such it would be, if the Sabbatical duty <note>This refers to the religious obligation to keep the Sabbath (Sunday) as a holy day of rest and worship.</note> was* *strictly kept up, with proper judgment. I am sure, 'tis* *the interest, safety, as well as duty of Government, to* *take effectual care about it. and the first step is to* *abrogate all public levees, <note>Formal morning receptions or assemblies held by the monarch or high-ranking nobility. Stukeley argues these should not take place on Sundays.</note> on that day: other enormitys <note>An older spelling of "enormities," meaning grave crimes or sins.</note> would dwindle of course.*

*The Sundays meditations <note>Refers to the specific essays or sermons contained in the following chapters of the book.</note> which follow, show how pro-* *fitably, and how agreeably, secular learning <note>Non-religious education, such as science, history, and classical literature.</note> may be em-* *ploy'd towards illustration of sacred history. when they* *go hand in hand, they inlarge <note>An older spelling of "enlarge."</note> the human mind; to that* *high spirit of universal benevolence, which makes every* *thing happy around us: which was the motive to Crea-* *tion; which exhibits that golden and beautiful medium,* <note>A reference to the "Golden Mean"—the ancient Greek philosophical ideal of the desirable middle ground between two extremes.</note>

<summary>The author argues that maintaining the sanctity of the Sabbath is essential for social order and government stability. He suggests that combining general education with religious study fosters a benevolent and enlightened mind.</summary>

# PREFACE.

*...in the ideas and practice of religion, equally free from superstition and fanaticism: which is the true glory and happiness of rational Beings.*

*The great principle of patriotism, <note>The author uses the phrase "love of one's country."</note> led me to always consider its original establishment. <note>The author uses the word "plantation," which in the 18th century referred to the settling or founding of a country.</note> The attached plan of chronology I believe to be a solid foundation upon which to build the <term>British Origins <note>original: "Origines Britanicæ"</note></term>, which I intend to publish next. Much of this is derived from the Scriptures; much from the oldest Greek poets. They had various reports concerning this from the Phoenician merchants in ancient times, though those merchants kept their knowledge as secret as possible.*

*I have engraved 150 of the ancient British coins, in all types of metal; being authentic historical evidence, <note>The author calls these "monuments," meaning physical objects or artifacts that serve as evidence of the past.</note> they provide us with a further framework on which to build our investigations into this most fascinating subject. I here request those individuals who have British coins in their collections <note>The text uses "cabinets," referring to "cabinets of curiosities" or private collections common among the gentry.</note> to favor me with them, so that I may engrave them or list their names alongside those already engraved. They may be certain that their coins will be carefully returned with proper thanks.*

# P R E F A C E.

...a proper spirit, in this first and most important article of religion; wherein the good, even of Society and Government, is so essentially concerned.

We need not wonder, at the absolute depravation of manners <note>Here, "manners" refers to moral conduct and social behavior.</note>, so very dangerous to public security; now let loose, among the **vulgar** <note>In the 18th century, "the vulgar" referred to the common people or those without a formal education.</note>. and I wish, the example was not wanting in those of superior class; to prevent the alarming danger of immorality, with open flood-gates rushing in upon us: owing to the egregious profanation <note>Specifically, the "profanation" or disrespect of the holy Sabbath day.</note>.

The whole course of learning is requisite, to the study of <term>Divinity <note>The study of religion and the nature of God; today more commonly called Theology.</note></term>, if we would exhibit its true grandeur, and genuine beauty. such has been my endeavor. for all studys should be directed to it; to the general and most effectual good of mankind: agreeable to the purpose of divine providence. he designed the world, for a paradise; and such it would be, if the **Sabbatical duty** <note>The religious obligation to keep the Sabbath (Sunday) as a day of rest and worship.</note> was strictly kept up, with proper judgment. I am sure, 'tis the interest, safety, as well as duty of Government, to take effectual care about it. and the first step is to abrogate <note>To abolish or do away with.</note> all **public levees** <note>A "levee" was a formal morning reception held by a person of high rank or a government official. Stukeley is criticizing the practice of holding high-society social gatherings on Sundays.</note>, on that day: other enormitys <note>An archaic spelling of "enormities," meaning grave sins or crimes.</note> would dwindle of course.

The Sundays meditations which follow, show how profitably, and how agreeably, **secular learning** <note>Non-religious academic subjects, such as history, science, and philosophy.</note> may be employ'd towards illustration of sacred history. when they go hand in hand, they inlarge the human mind; to that high spirit of **universal benevolence**, which makes every thing happy around us: which was the motive to Creation; which exhibits that golden and beautiful medium,

<meta>This is the first page of the manuscript, beginning the commentary on the Book of Genesis. The catchword at the bottom of the original page is "Herein."</meta>

# I.

GENESIS 1:11. *And God said, let the earth bring forth grass, the herb yielding seed, and the fruit tree yielding fruit after his kind; whose seed is in its self, upon the earth; and it was so.*

THIS first chapter of *Genesis* is the most noble, the most sublime piece of writing that ever proceeded from the pen of a mortal; and it sufficiently proclaims itself to be dictated by a divine spirit.

The language is so exquisite that it moved the greatest critic among the ancients, *Longinus* <note>Dionysius Cassius Longinus (c. 213–273 AD), a Greek philosopher and rhetorician who famously praised the Hebrew lawgiver in his work "On the Sublime."</note>, to give it extraordinary high praise <note>original: "encomium"</note>. So accurate is the philosophy contained within it that it is most worthy of admiration, even among those who have drunk deeply from the fountain of *Newtonian* <note>Referring to the physics and natural philosophy of Sir Isaac Newton.</note> science.

*In the beginning God created the heaven, and the earth.* That is to say, the matter or substance of which the world is made.

*And the spirit of God moved upon the face of the* inactive, cold, and dark matter, called *Waters*, <term>Mót<gloss>A word, likely of Phoenician origin, referring to a watery "mud" or primordial substance from which the universe was thought to have formed.</gloss></term> or mud; a fluid, confused jumble of the parts of everything that was created.

<summary>This page introduces a commentary on the first chapter of Genesis, praising its literary sublimity and its compatibility with the scientific views of the Enlightenment, specifically those of Isaac Newton. The author interprets the primordial "waters" as a physical substance or "mud" from which all matter originated.</summary>

<keywords>Genesis, Longinus, Newtonian physics, Creation, Matter, Primeval Mud, Mót, Biblical Commentary</keywords>

--- Page 23 ---
<meta>This page continues the commentary on Genesis, picking up from the description of the primordial matter. The catchword from the previous page, "He," begins the first word "Herein."</meta>

Herein is understood the universal principle of gravitation, or attraction which was impressed <note>original: "imprest"</note> upon the vast mass. We can very easily think of, but not fully grasp <note>original: "comprehend"</note>, the consequences of the immense agitation or fermentation of the different component parts of its substance.

Moses informs us of it, in the next verse.

> *God said, let there be light; and there was light.*

This is that famous verse which Longinus the Critic <note>The Greek rhetorician mentioned on the previous page who cited this verse as a prime example of the "sublime" in writing.</note> so much admires.

It means that God separated the ignited <term>ignifyed<gloss>Derived from the Latin 'ignis' (fire); meaning turned into fire or made glowing with heat.</gloss></term> part of matter, heated to the utmost degree; and from it formed the body of the sun: which was now to assist in perfecting his great work.

In the second day, he made the earth, and planets, attendant upon this sun.

In the third day, he formed the sea and the dry land, then follow the words of our text.

> *And God said, let the earth bring forth grass, the herb yielding seed, and the fruit tree yielding fruit, after his kind; whose seed is in its self, upon the earth, and it was so. and God saw that it was good.*

i. e. <note>An abbreviation for the Latin 'id est,' meaning 'that is to say.'</note> it was complete, fair and beautiful, exactly conforming to the divine idea in his mind. An all-perfect copy, from the most excellent original!

And indeed, we may with some reason judge that our Founder, Mr. Fairchild <note>Thomas Fairchild (1667–1729), a celebrated gardener and benefactor who established an annual lecture—often called the "Vegetable Sermon"—to celebrate God's creation of plants.</note>, was fully sensible of this. The vegetable world was his province. It was his study, as well as his maintenance. He every day saw, and admired its beauties, and adored the great author of such admirable, such inconceivable perfection.

<summary>The author interprets the creation of light as the formation of the sun from "ignified" matter and explains how the third day of creation resulted in a perfect "copy" of God's divine plan. He links these biblical events to the life of Thomas Fairchild, a gardener who studied the botanical world as an act of worship.</summary>

<keywords>gravitation, Genesis 1:3, Genesis 1:11, the Sun, Thomas Fairchild, Fairchild Lecture, botany, divine design, Longinus, creationism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the introduction to the annual "Vegetable Sermon," transitioning from the theological description of Creation to the legacy of the lecture's founder, Thomas Fairchild, and the specific outline of the speaker's upcoming discourse.</meta>

<page-num>3</page-num>
<sig>B 2</sig>
<warning>Slight show-through of text from the verso page.</warning>

He <note>Referring to Thomas Fairchild (1667–1729), the gardener who founded this lecture series.</note>, like our once happy parent and forefather <note>A reference to Adam in the Garden of Eden.</note>, lived in a garden—but he was far happier, for he kept possession of his home and maintained his innocence.

Not content with enjoying such happiness <term>felicity<gloss>Deep happiness or bliss.</gloss></term> all his life long, he gave from his own wealth to ensure that others could continue to enjoy that same pleasure. It is this gift that calls us together annually, just as we are *here this day*.

To ensure there was no interruption in his praiseworthy and religious <term>pious<gloss>Devoutly religious or motivated by duty to God.</gloss></term> purpose, I agreed to this task, though I am well aware of the difficulty of following after the *one* <note>Likely referring to a previous long-standing lecturer of the Fairchild Trust.</note> who, for so many years together, had performed it with such elegance and skill.

And it is also primarily thanks to Dr. Denne <note>Dr. John Denne (1693–1767), the Vicar of St. Leonard’s, Shoreditch, who was instrumental in managing the Fairchild trust.</note>, the Vicar of this church, that the fund for supporting this lecture was established and increased.

I shall speak, first, of the beauty and the usefulness of the plant world in general.

Second, I shall address a specific aspect of its use from a religious perspective, and *that* from the most ancient times.

Third, I shall highlight one significant science derived from the plant world: the origin of architecture.

### I. Regarding the beauty and the usefulness of the plant world in general.

Whatever proceeds from the shaping hand of the great and all-wise Creator must necessarily be highly beautiful and *most exquisite*! And in the ordered progression <term>gradation<gloss>A series of successive stages or degrees.</gloss></term> of the *Creation of the world* <note>original: "Cosmogony"; the study of the origin of the universe.</note>—

[cos]mogony <note>The text begins mid-word, completing "cosmogony," which refers to a theory or story regarding the origin of the universe.</note> recited in the first chapter of *Genesis*, we observe in how orderly and truly philosophical a view it proceeds, to the third day of creation; when the earth is separated from the ocean, and dry land appears. The same day is the earth clothed with the vegetable world. For on the next, or fourth day, the earth, along with the other planets, are set in their proper orbs <note>The circular paths or orbits of celestial bodies.</note>, around the sun; and have performed a revolution, and constituted the fourth day.

Then was its surface sufficiently dry, to bear the animals; who were made on the fifth day. And then had the plants time to vegetate, and provide sustenance, for the animals next to be formed. *The common father of the universe <note>A reference to God as the creator.</note> beneficently spread a sufficient, a various, a splendid table, for his infinite family;* before he brought them into being. *He opened his hand wide, and filled them with food, and gladness <note>A paraphrase of Psalm 145:16: "Thou openest thine hand, and satisfiest the desire of every living thing."</note>.* That they might rejoice in their recent being; and each, and all in their way, *praise the goodness of a kind maker*, who had *given them all things richly to enjoy;* who *bid them live, and be happy;* and *envied not* <note>Meaning God was not begrudging or stingy with His creation.</note>.

But to speak fully, and pertinently in general, of the beauty, the use of the vegetable world, requires the elocution <note>The skill of clear and expressive speech.</note> and the wisdom of a *Solomon*: *Solomon* the wisest of mortals, that ever was born! But how much does it enhance the glory of the vegetable world, that the wisdom of *Solomon* was not complete; till he wrote of trees, *from the cedar tree that was in Lebanon, even unto the hyssop, that springeth out of the wall <note>This refers to 1 Kings 4:33, describing Solomon's vast botanical knowledge, ranging from the grandest trees to the smallest plants.</note>*.

These he studied in his exquisite garden, at a fountain of waters, between Bethlehem, and Jerusalem;

> *I made myself pools of water to water the forest of growing trees.*

A view of *Solomon's* garden and pools can be found in *le Brun’s* travels <note>Referring to the Dutch artist and traveler Cornelis de Bruyn (1652–1726), who published famous illustrated accounts of the Levant.</note>.

There, amid shrubs, trees, and flowers, he composed his admirable songs; breathing pure nature in its best attire. These were the original source of *Greek* and *Latin* pastoral <note>A genre of literature, often poetry, that idealizes rural life and landscapes.</note> poetry; but scarcely can the *Greek* and *Latin* works match the delicacy of the East. These are vividly bright—nature in its most refined state. From such views as he had from his rural retreat there, or at the tower of Mount *Lebanon* <note>Original: "Libanus."</note>; there the Muse <note>The personification of poetic inspiration.</note> left *Parnassus* <note>A mountain in Greece sacred to the Muses; the author implies Solomon’s setting was more inspiring than the traditional home of Greek poetry.</note> behind.

> *I am the rose of Sharon,* says he, *and the lily of the valleys. Look from the top of Amana; from the top of Senir and Hermon.* <note>Geographic landmarks mentioned in the Song of Solomon.</note>

Behind him were the great forests of Cedar; before him, the delightful prospect over *Damascus*, the finest country on earth.

[Cos]mogeny <note>Completing the word "cosmogony," meaning an account or theory regarding the origin of the universe, from the previous page.</note> recited in the first chapter of *Genesis*, we observe in how orderly and truly philosophical a view it proceeds, to the third day of creation; when the earth is separated from the ocean, and dry land appears. In the country, we never find a *little* cottage, without a *little* garden. the sweetness, the simplicity; the fragrance, no less than the use, affects unsophisticate nature. the morning dew, the noon-tide serenity, the evening odors, give a delight, to be felt, better than described.

From hence the enchanting words of tranquillity, of serene, of the contemplative, raise the sensible mind into its raptures, its most agreeable reveries. the brighter the soul, the more it relishes those incomparable pleasures; the more it adores the infinite source of all beauty, the *great creator*!

*There* harmony, and music excites every sense into complacency, and becomes rival to celestial melody; when in sight of green fields, <gloss>and wood,</gloss> it is join’d, with the aerial concert of the winged choir <note>A poetic reference to the singing of birds.</note>.

We observe in all writing, all history, the greatest genius, tho’ never so late, retiring, or hoping to retire, to the sweet, rural solitude, in the fulness of days <note>A biblical idiom referring to reaching a ripe old age or the end of a long life.</note>.

Admirers of flowers, regretting the short-liv’d beauty of these curious forms, may with reason be thought the authors of the noble arts of drawing and painting, to preserve and relieve the vicissitudes of nature.

<meta>This page continues the author’s discussion on how art and architecture derive their perfection from imitating nature, specifically focusing on the botanical designs used in the construction of King Solomon's Temple.</meta>

in proportion to their adapting their skill, their invention, their imitation to the perfection of nature; in the disposition, the colors, the varieties, of these incomparable beauties.

The vegetable productions furnish out the materials, and the forms of architecture. Thus the cedar pillars of *Solomon’s* <note>Solomon was the third king of Israel (c. 970–931 BC), renowned for his wisdom and for building the First Temple in Jerusalem.</note> temple had capitals of *lily work*, of palm branches, of pomegranates. His vast molten sea <note>A large bronze basin in the Temple courtyard used by priests for ritual washing.</note> had two rows of pomegranates, round the verge; and the brim was wrought with flowers of *lilies*. He cast many brazen vases on bases with wheels, for the use of the temple service. Among other devices, they were ornamented with palm branches.

The candlesticks of pure gold, to be set within the temple, were of *flower work*. The pillars, pilasters, the ceiling and wainscotting, was of cedar beams, and boards. So that the very smell of the building was the most delightful imaginable. The floors were of the same materials. And all was carved with hexagonal network, like that of a honeycomb; every cell filled with *cherubim* <note>Winged celestial beings who serve as attendants to God; in the Temple, they were depicted in carvings and as large statues.</note>, fruits, and flowers, disposed in the most elegant taste. It is fit we should commemorate the royal founder of botany, in that pretty plant called *Solomon’s seal* <note>A flowering plant (genus *Polygonatum*) whose roots bear scars resembling ancient wax seals; the author credits Solomon as the "founder of botany" based on biblical accounts of his vast knowledge of plant life.</note>.

Thus we see fruits and flowers introduced as fit concomitants <note>Naturally accompanying or associated things.</note> to the heavenly inhabitants, the *cherubim!* and all these overlaid in the most exquisite manner, with purest gold. Too great cost could not be bestowed on these imitations of nature’s beauties. This was an earthly *Tabernacle* <note>A sacred dwelling place or sanctuary.</note>, was a house, to be honored with the presence of the *supreme*, the invisible deity! so that the

->*The gods also inhabited the forests.* <note>original: "Habitarunt Dii quoque Sylvas." A quote from the Roman poet Virgil’s *Eclogues* (7.60), often used to justify the antiquity and naturalness of forest worship.</note><-

II. And this naturally brings us, to the second head of our discourse, to treat of a particular branch of the vegetable world used in matters of religion; and *that* from highest antiquity. From a tree comes the frankincense, the odorous *incense* <note>original: "suffimen," a Latin term for a substance burned to create a fragrant smoke for ritual purification or offering.</note>, the morning sacrifice of all mankind, derived from the practice of the earliest times.

It was ever the practice of the old world, to use flowers, and branches, in all great acts of religion. They wore garlands of flowers on their heads, and leafy crowns of plants, in token of festivity. For in scripture language the highest acts of religion were called *rejoicing before the Lord*. And so in fact, those solemnities were designed to be pictures and representations of heavenly felicity. And so our sublimest acts of religion really are, the **Eucharistic celebration**; the fulfilling of the ancient ones; a feast on the sacrifice. Religion was not intended to make us melancholy, but cheerful.

Rejoicing especially was the practice, on the more solemn, and festival performances; at public sacrifice, which they called *Religious Assemblies* <note>original: "Panegyres," from the Greek term for a general assembly or national festival held in honor of a god.</note>; a meeting of a side of a country, or a province. This was done four times in the

<meta>This page continues the author's exploration of ancient religious rituals involving the natural world. Having previously discussed sacred groves and the "patriarchal Druidry" of Abraham, the author now transitions from the spaces of worship to the specific timing and floral decorations of religious festivals, linking pagan and Druidical customs to modern Christian celebrations.</meta>

<page-num>9</page-num>

year; on what we call quarter days <note>In the British Isles, quarter days were the four dates in each year on which traditionally servants were hired, and rents were due: Lady Day, Midsummer, Michaelmas, and Christmas.</note>, the Equinoxes, and Solstices.

Equivalent to these, are our present Christian festivals: Christmas, Easter, Whitsuntide <note>Also known as Pentecost, falling seven weeks after Easter.</note>, and Michaelmas <note>The Feast of Saints Michael, Gabriel, and Raphael, celebrated on September 29th near the autumnal equinox.</note>.

At these times, both priests and people not only wore garlands of flowers on their heads, but likewise adorned the animals to be sacrificed with chaplets <note>A wreath or circular decoration for the head.</note> and festoons of the same; as well as the pillars of their temples, the altars, and the like. Our Druids, who always bore staves in their hands, twisted them about with similar festoons.

All this we see in innumerable sculptures of antiquity: vine branches, ivy, oak-leaves, pines, cypress, palm branches, and so on. Furthermore, we observe in the heathen world that they not only had particular branches for each particular deity, but likewise distinct foliages, festoons, and flowers for particular seasons or quarters of the year. This usage is still kept up in some measure in our churches at this day, at Easter, Whitsuntide, and Christmas time.

This is derived, in great measure, from our Druids (who were of the true religion) as well as from the practices of the heathen, transmitted from the most ancient times. A detail of this piece of religious history, holding forth the glories of the vegetable world, is very curious because it is often overlooked; and it will fill up the remainder of our time.

We must first state that all nations upon earth had an expectation of that person whom we call *Messiah*. The learned *Huetius* <note>Pierre Daniel Huet (1630–1721), a French bishop and scholar known for his work "Demonstratio Evangelica," which sought to prove the truth of Christianity through historical and comparative study of all cultures.</note> shows that even the *Chinese* philosopher *Confucius* expected this illustrious advent <note>The arrival or "coming" of a significant person.</note>.

The Druids held it for a symbol of the *Messiah*, whom they expected to be born at this *winter Solstice*; when mistletoe, by the wonderful order of nature—contrary to other plants—is in its full bloom and utmost perfection. <note>Mistletoe is unusual because it stays green and bears fruit in the dead of winter when its host tree appears lifeless.</note>

That our Saviour was truly born at the *winter Solstice*, where we now celebrate it, admits, in my judgment, of a divine proof. It is deduced from the prophets celebrating that great advent <note>The arrival or birth of Christ.</note> with a rehearsal of evergreens to grace the *salutiferous* <note>original: "salutiferous," meaning health-bringing or bringing salvation.</note> solemnity. In conformity to which, we at this day adorn our churches with them.

*Isaiah* LX. 1. *Arise, shine, for thy light is come* etc. The prophet calls upon the sun, at its utmost elongation <note>The point where the sun is furthest from the celestial equator; the solstice.</note>, to advance and lengthen out the day to accompany the divine birth.

> *The glory of Lebanon shall come unto thee; the fir-tree, the pine, and the box together; to beautify the place of my sanctuary; and I will make the place of my foot glorious.*

meaning the birth of the *Messiah*.

*Isaiah* XLI. 19. On a similar occasion:

> *The cedar, shittah <term>shittah: The wood of the acacia tree, often mentioned in the Bible as the material for the Ark of the Covenant.</term>, myrtle, olive, fir, pine, and box are gathered together.*

*Isaiah* LV. 13.

> *Instead of the thorn, shall come up the fir-tree; instead of the brier, shall spring the myrtle.*

meaning the kingdom of the *Messiah*.

In my opinion, *Isaiah* alludes to the mistletoe in that obscure and corrupt passage which commentators avoid. His meaning is to make the plant symbolic of the *Messiah* and of Christianity, to be inoculated <note>In a gardening sense, this refers to grafting a bud from one plant onto another. Hence the old Romans called the winter Solstice *Saturnalia*, the great birthday of the invincible *Mithras* <note>A sun god of Persian origin whose festival on December 25th was later superseded by Christmas.</note>, who was to restore the *Saturnian* age <note>A mythical golden age of peace and abundance.</note>. The Druids called it *Yule*, which signifies the festival, by way of eminence <note>Meaning it was considered the festival above all others.</note>. Thus far as to Christmastide.

The festival of the vernal Equinox was known from prophetic notices to be the time of the death of *Messiah*, and celebrated in a manner suited thereto; but the reason was obscured and forgotten. The plants of this season used on the festival by all nations were the *hyacinth* kind, and the *anemone* kind, of infinite beauty and variety, together with the *flower of Adonis* <note>original: "flos adonidis"; likely referring to the pheasant's eye or a similar red flower associated with the blood of the mythical Adonis.</note>: all plants of the time. Violets, the *three-colored violet* <note>original: "viola tricolor," the wild pansy or heartsease.</note>.

The true purport <note>The intended meaning or purpose.</note> of the festival was by the poets wrought into fables of *Adonis* killed, among the Syrian nations. and an anniversary lamentation was made for him by the women for three days together. After that, a great rejoicing for him come to life again. This is hinted at in *Jeremiah* VII. 29: *a lamentation in high places*, meaning the heathen temples.

All this was done at the vernal equinox, our Easter time, a name borrowed from the Syrian goddess *Astarte* <note>An ancient Semitic goddess of fertility and beauty, often associated with the planet Venus.</note>, mother of *Adonis*. At last, *Adonis* is turned into the flower of this season, the *anemone*; the flower with which they made their crowns, garlands, and festoons for the sacred solemnity; for their heads, their staves, for the victims, temples, and altars.

<meta>This page continues the exploration of botanical symbolism in ancient religion, transitioning from the myths associated with the vernal equinox to the plants sacred to the Druids during the summer solstice. It connects classical mythology to "sacred notices" or prophecies of the Messiah.</meta>

turned the youth into this flower <note>Referring to the myth of Hyacinthus, a youth loved by Apollo who was accidentally killed and transformed into the hyacinth flower.</note>. These are ancient religious truths, turned into fables by the devil's craft and human weakness, in order to obscure the sacred prophecies of the *Messiah* suffering; through the names of *Hyacinthus*, *Anemone*, *Adonis*, and the flowers used upon the occasion. For this reason, a favorite of *Apollo*, *Cyparissus* <note>In Greek myth, a youth who was transformed into a cypress tree, a symbol of mourning.</note>, was turned by him into that mournful cypress plant. So *Attis* <note>A Phrygian deity of vegetation; his self-mutilation and rebirth under a pine tree were celebrated in spring festivals.</note>, beloved of the mother of the gods, was represented as crucified on a pine. This was the Easter solemnity. Thus far for the flowers of the vernal equinox.

The famous plant, the beautiful foxglove, is one of the glories of the summer solstice: the high favorite of the Druids, both for its very powerful medicinal virtues and for its great elegance and duration, gradually growing taller on a noble stem. I have sometimes observed it to bloom on the very day of the solstice. This was the ornament of this quarterly sacrifice. It has its name from the Druids: *fees, fairies, folks, popelli* <note>Latin: "the common people." The author suggests "foxglove" is a corruption of "folk's glove," referring to the "good folk" or fairies.</note>; notions among the common people, transmitted down from their times. The form of the flower, its purple color and embroidery, resembles their priestly caps, or mitres; like those of the *Persians* and *Phrygians*. They were the patriarchal, oriental bonnet.

*Hierobotane* <note>Greek: "Sacred plant."</note>, the sacred plant, *peristerion* <note>Greek: "Pigeon-herb."</note>, *vervain*, or pigeon-herb, so named from its current flowering: pigeons being now the usual sacrifice. It is peculiar as being then in its highest perfection; it does not come from any fancy of the pigeons being fond of the herb. This was reckoned a magical—that is, a Druidical—plant. Our *aquilegia* <note>Latin: Columbine.</note>, or columbine, was now also used.

<meta>This page continues the botanical and ritual survey of the Druidic year, moving from the summer solstice traditions mentioned on the previous page to the significance of the autumnal equinox and the sacred nature of the oak tree.</meta>

<page-num>16</page-num>

*St. John's Wort* <note>original: *Hypericon*</note>, called the *expeller of demons* <note>original Latin: *fuga dæmonum*</note>, is reckoned among sacred magical plants on account of the Druids using them; it is considered good against witchcraft.

At the summer solstice, they used likewise to make wreaths and chaplets of our native *greater* and *lesser bindweed* <note>original Latin: *convolvulus major* and *minor*</note>, a beautiful white bell-shaped flower <term>campaniform: shaped like a bell, from the Latin 'campana'</term>, growing plentifully at this season. Hence the magical notion became affixed to them by the common people, who called them *spirits' bells*.

The autumnal equinox was celebrated with the oak, then heavy with acorns; a tree, from all antiquity thought most sacred; and with which I shall conclude this discourse.

*Maximus of Tyre* <note>A second-century Platonic philosopher known for his lectures on theological and philosophical topics.</note>, a famous *Platonist*, informs us the Druids worshipped *Jupiter* <note>The Roman king of the gods, whom the author equates with the Druidic supreme deity</note>; whose statue or sign, says he, was a very high oak tree. We are not to be moved to think the Druids were idolaters. The truth is this: the great woods and groves were their green temples at this season of the year; the boughs of oak and acorns were the ornaments of their staffs and altars; which they cut down with the brazen instruments called *Celts* <term>Celts: a term used by early antiquarians to describe ancient bronze axe-heads or chisels found in the soil</term>. Innumerable quantities of these we find at this day in Britain and the surrounding islands. But they preserved the custom of the East, from whence they came, of having a *focal point* <note>original: *kebla*, an Arabic term (Qibla) referring to the direction faced during prayer; the author uses it to suggest a Middle-Eastern origin for British rituals</note>, or object to which they all turned their faces in acts of religion. It is the Arabian method to this day. From there, the Druids and the original Britons came.

In the open temples of the Druids, they had an upright stone <note>original: *obeliscal stone*</note> set upright for the *focal point*; or three stones set in the manner of a niche <note>original: *nich-wise*</note>, symbolic of the divine presence. In a

grove, they chose out a handsome oak, with two cross-like branches. On the stem of the tree, they inscribed the word TARAN <note>Taranis was the Celtic god of thunder, often associated by 18th-century scholars with the supreme sky-deity.</note>, which signifies God the supreme; above and below, the word THAV <note>Possibly a reference to the Hebrew letter 'Tau', which some antiquarians believed represented the name of God among ancient peoples.</note>, which signifies deity. On the cross-arm to the right, the word BELEN <note>Belenus was a major Celtic sun god, frequently compared to the Greek Apollo.</note>, meaning the all-healing Savior. On the left arm, the word HES <note>Often associated with Esus, a powerful Celtic deity; here the author interprets him as a "divine spirit."</note>, meaning the divine spirit.

Thus they endeavored to picture out the nature of the godhead: thus, as we Christians, they worshiped the three divine personalities, in one deity.

### III.
We have, in some measure, celebrated the beauties, uses and the glories, of the vegetable world. We have more particularly shown, its subserviency to religion. Nothing, no learning, no science, no great purpose in life, is of value, that has not some regard thereto. We conclude, with the origin and use of sacred groves, from a letter of *Cicero's* <note>Marcus Tullius Cicero (106–43 BC), the Roman statesman and philosopher.</note> to *Atticus*, about his building a temple, for a sort of apotheosis <term>apotheosis: the glorification of a subject to divine level; in this case, Cicero's attempt to deify his deceased daughter.</term> to his daughter *Tullia*; we learn thence, that groves for religion, are more ancient <note>original: "antienter"</note> than their covered temples; like those of the Greeks and Romans, encompassed with pillars. Indeed these latter sort of temples, are strictly but imitations of groves. And groves, we may conclude, gave occasion to the invention of architecture; especially that first sort; which is the most ancient. In Homer is frequent mention of religious groves, no temples. These groves for religious use all nations took from that of ABRAHAM <note>The author refers to the biblical "Groves of Mamre," suggesting that all ancient religious traditions, including those of the Druids, descended from the Hebrew patriarchs.</note>. Our Druids too from him.

From these groves arose the first ideas of architecture. First was it employed for sacred purpose. And the first kind, or Order of architecture, was *that*, we erroneously—

<meta>This page concludes the author's discussion on the origins of religious architecture, transitioning from the previous page's mention of Cicero's temples to a theory that "Gothic" architecture is actually an "Arabian" style designed to mimic the sacred groves of the Druids and the ancient Mosaic Tabernacle.</meta>

<page-num>18</page-num>
<sig>C</sig>

...erroneously <note>The text begins mid-word, likely continuing "erroneously" from the previous page.</note> call Gothic. It is truly Arabian: it came from Arabia, when covered temples were built, following the model of the *Mosaic* Tabernacle <note>The portable sanctuary tent used by the Israelites during the Exodus, which the author views as the prototype for all sacred architecture.</note>.

Such is the fabric of our ancient churches and cathedrals. The slender pillars imitate the tapering trunk of a tree. The curve of the arches is derived from the delicate branching of the boughs, in a wood, or grove. The mullioned lacework <note>The decorative masonry or "tracery" that supports the glass in a window.</note> of the windows does the same, intercepting the dubious light <note>Meaning dim or flickering light.</note>, just as in a real grove. Here silence reigns, except for the agreeable murmurs of the wind overhead; here the gloomy obscurity leads the mind into a profound and solemn seriousness, a collection of thought, exciting that awe and veneration proper for the temples of the living GOD.

When we add painted glass to our churches, every sense is concentrated into the contemplative.

I have shown in chapter XV. of *Abury* <note>Referring to the author's own work, *Abury, a Temple of the British Druids* (1743), which interpreted the stone circles of Avebury as ancient religious sites.</note>, that the Druids, as well as those in the first ages of the world, were acquainted with the divine geniture <note>The theological concept of the "eternal generation" or the begetting of the Son by the Father.</note>, and the emanation from the supreme fountain of being.

As once of old in groves, so here in their representative fabrics <note>Meaning buildings that represent or stand in for the original groves.</note>, we adore the three sacred persons of the deity, Father, Son, and Holy Ghost, to whom be ascribed all honour and glory, now and for ever. Amen.

->**GENESIS I. 11.** *And the earth brought forth grass, and herb yielding seed, after his kind; and the tree yielding fruit, whose seed was in its self, after his kind, and God saw that it was good.*<-

THIS was on the third day of *creation*. In the *first day*, the divine *architect* <note>A common Enlightenment-era metaphor for God, viewing the Creator as a master builder or engineer of the universe.</note>, proposing to build a *world*, provides materials; like a wise artist. He created the fund of matter, of which the world was to be made. This he created properly speaking; he produced it out of nothing; out of the infinite *vacuum* <note>original Latin: *vacuum*, meaning an empty space or void. It is the greatest absurdity in the world, to suppose it pre-existent, and coeternal with the *deity*.

In this *first day*, he produced fire and light; the consequence of the motion of the divine *spirit* on the *face* of the *waters*, as it is called, the *chaos* <note>original Greek: *chaos*, referring to the formless, void state of the universe before the intervention of the creator.</note>; which put matter into a most prodigious <note>Meaning immense or marvelous.</note> *fermentation*: an *intestine motion* <note>A term from early modern physics referring to the internal agitation or movement of particles within a substance, which was thought to generate heat.</note>, or *heat*. It was an *heterogeneous collection* <note>A mixture of many different, unorganized substances.</note> of all kind of different *substances*, fermenting, which must produce *fire*. GOD divided the *light* from the *darkness*.

No sooner did the mountains *lift up* their heads, and the land emerge out of the boundless ocean; but the universal face of the earth was overspread, and clothed, in its green attire. The kind, the *beneficent* parent of the universe <note>God.</note>, took care to provide food for his numerous family <note>All the living creatures of the earth.</note>; which he was about to bring into being. He spread a table for them ready, before they wanted it. He did it not, in a niggardly <note>In a stingy or meager fashion.</note> manner, but wherever the animal could tread, *there* found he plenty of nourishment under his feet. He could only stoop, and take it.

A consideration of the amazing variety of plants is a topic, that entertains botanists, in a high degree. It entertains them in their mutual discourse and conversation; in their elaborate studies, concerning them; and in their silent and solitary contemplations upon them: such as our ingenious founder <note>Likely referring to the person who established the specific botanical garden or institution associated with this manuscript.</note> enjoy’d, in the midst of a garden, when he sat, overlooking, ordering his plants, brought from *every quarter of the globe*; breathing his *ardent ejaculations* <note>In this context, "ejaculations" refers to short, sudden, and fervent prayers or outbursts of devotion.</note> from a pious soul; touch’d with devoutest praises to the almighty creator; whose invention so much exceeds all possible human conception!

How did our founder enjoy himself, reflecting on the exceeding dignity of his profession. *God*, not only planted a garden in Eden, but set the plants in it himself. *for God made every plant of the earth, before it was in the earth; and every herb of the field, before it grew.* <note>A paraphrase of Genesis 2:4–5, which the author interprets to mean that the design or "spirit" of the plants was created by God before they physically sprouted from the soil.</note>

a faint idea, but a most striking picture, of the long sleep of the squalid <term>squalid: here meaning dark, neglected, or dismal</term> grave; and at length of a certain resurrection to new glories. In no other way than among animals that sleep over the winter, they remain in a torpid <term>torpid: a state of physical inactivity or dormancy, like hibernation</term> state, till the new spring returns them to new life and vigor. 

The spring flowers, *monkshood* <note>original: "aconitum"</note>, snowdrops, and the like, lie dead all summer; so does the autumnal saffron <note>likely referring to the Meadow Saffron or Autumn Crocus</note>, etc.

### II.
This naturally brings us to the second contemplation: that exact likeness of a future life, exhibited to us from the vegetable world; with which we shall conclude the present discourse.

Life is motion; both animal life and vegetable life. Motion is of two kinds: that in straight lines (progressive, planetary, or cometary motion) and that motion within itself (internal <note>original: "intestin"</note>, fermentation, or vibration). In vibration, the vegetative life evidently consists. Motion may be infinitely quick, like the rays of light, flame, lightning, or electricity—or that of a comet at its *point nearest the sun* <note>original: "perihelion"</note>—or infinitely slow and absolutely imperceptible, as is the vibration in the parts of plants when in sleep or at rest; or moderately quick, as in electrical vibration or glowing-hot iron.

Thus, an instance: an acquaintance dug up a potato bed and turned it into a grass plot. Twenty-two years after, his successor converted it back into garden ground. It was soon overgrown with potatoes. 

Another friend of mine sowed some cucumber seed which had lain by for thirty-three years, wrapped up in paper. It produced fine fruit.

a faint idea, but a most striking picture, of the long sleep of the squalid grave; and at length of a certain resurrection to new glories. It is no different than among animals that sleep over the winter; they remain in a torpid state, until the new spring returns them to new life and vigor.

The spring flowers, monkshood <note>original: "*aconitum*"</note>, snowdrops, and the like, lie dead all summer; so does the autumnal saffron, and others.

II. This naturally brings us to the second contemplation, that exact likeness of a future life, exhibited to us from the vegetable world; with which we shall conclude the present discourse.

Life is motion; both animal life and vegetable life. Motion is of two kinds: that in straight lines, which is progressive, planetary, and cometary motion; and that motion within itself, which is internal <note>original: "intestin"</note>, a fermentation or vibration. In vibration, the vegetative life evidently consists. Motion may be infinitely quick, like the rays of light, flame, lightning, or electricity: or that of a comet in its *perihelion* <term>perihelion: the point in the orbit of a planet or comet where it is closest to the sun</term>: or infinitely slow, and absolutely imperceptible, as is the vibration in the parts of plants when in sleep, or at rest: or moderately quick, as in the electrical vibration or ignited iron.

Thus, for instance: an acquaintance dug up a potato bed and turned it into a grass plot. Twenty-two years after, his successor converted it back into garden ground. It was soon overgrown with potatoes.

Another friend of mine sowed some cucumber seed, which had lain by for thirty-three years, wrapped up in paper. It produced fine fruit.

<meta>catchword: which</meta>

There is a universal and established law in creation that completely excludes spontaneous generation <note>the now-disproven theory that life could arise from non-living matter, such as dust or decaying flesh; original: "equivocal generation"</note> in plants, as well as in animals!

The herb yields seed according to its kind, and the tree yields seed within its respective fruit. Just as a plant derives its being from a distinct and perpetual seed, it likewise provides the same source for its succeeding offspring. The same principle holds true for animals.

Hence, an eternal provision is made by our all-good Creator for a constant succession of all the species of beings which He made at the beginning; no new species can appear through any spontaneous generation.

In a discussion of plants, permit me to honor the memory of that great genius, my esteemed friend Dr. Stephen Hales <note>1677–1761, a famous clergyman and scientist who pioneered the study of plant physiology and air chemistry</note>, who has served the botanical world so well, and from whom I first gained a love for scientific <note>original: "philosophical"</note> studies.

How we must admire the absolute harmony and agreement between the divine philosophy of Moses and of St. Paul! It is so perfectly consistent with our current biological <note>original: "physiological"</note> systems.

But let us follow St. Paul, who carries us beyond our current biological systems with a most elevated spirit of truly divine philosophy. He leads us toward the regions of a future life and to a state of glorious immortality. This is the true goal of all our studies and inquiries. In this life, we make only a momentary show—a colorful appearance—but like *the flower of the field, we soon pass away, and at every station, our place knows us no more* <note>a reference to Psalm 103:15-16</note>.

## III.

**GENESIS I. 11.**
> *And GOD said, let the earth bring forth grass, the herb yielding seed, and the fruit tree yielding fruit, after his kind; whose seed is in its self, upon the earth: and it was so.*

I Have spoke in this place already, of the majesty, and sublimity <note>In this context, sublimity refers to a quality of such greatness or beauty that it inspires overwhelming awe.</note> of this first chapter of *Genesis*: and that ’tis truly philosophical <note>The author argues that the biblical account of creation is not merely a story, but is consistent with the logical principles of "natural philosophy," which we would today call science.</note>.

GOD, the eternal first cause <term>first cause: a philosophical concept referring to the initial power or being that started the universe; a role traditionally assigned to the Creator</term>, created matter out of nothing; as the *materials* of his work. he put it into motion, like the heat of a ferment <note>The author compares the early state of creation to "fermentation," a process of internal heat and chemical change that leads to growth or ripening.</note>; for its maturation <term>maturation: the process of developing or ripening until reaching a finished state</term>. he separated it, heated, into its distinct allotments; at length, he adorned it, in all its parts; till the mighty work of creation was perfected. and he pronounced his note of approbation <term>approbation: formal praise or an expression of warm approval</term>; *he saw, that it was good. he saw every thing that he had made; and behold, it was very good. and the evening and the morning, were the sixth day.*

GOD almighty was not only a skilful, but an orderly workman; to teach us, to use order, and method, and regularity, in all our works. for we may say with great propriety, he plan’d it, and adorn’d; he laid it out, like a beautiful garden, and then planted it.

This was not all; but the highest and most significant purpose was achieved through it: in observing the order of the days' works. It is a purpose most relevant to us—I mean the institution of the Sabbath, that *high*, most venerable, most *holy* day; the crowning achievement of all His work, the most resplendent jewel of creation! It is the final touch, the finishing ornament of it all.

For what would this whole magnificent structure, the world, have been worth without the Sabbath? The holy author <note>original: "penman"; a traditional way of referring to Moses as the inspired writer of the first five books of the Bible.</note> would have considered his account of Creation incomplete without recording it in such a magnificent manner.

*Thus the heavens, and the earth were finished; and all the host of them.* <note>The "host" refers to the vast array of stars, angels, and all creatures that inhabit the heavens and the earth.</note>

*And on the seventh day, GOD ended his work, which he had made. and he rested on the seventh day, from all his work which he had made.*

*And GOD blessed the seventh day, and sanctified it; because that in it, he had rested from all his work, which GOD created, and made.*

I have spent so much time on this topic because it should be the first and most important thought that can enter into the heart of a thinking person. The world and all its shining, natural beauties were created in vain without a proper regard for morality—the law governing the rational part of His creation. And how is that morality to be maintained except through a careful observance of the sabbatical duty <term>sabbatical duty: the religious and moral obligation to keep the seventh day holy as a time of rest and worship</term>? This alone creates and preserves that *impression* of religion upon the minds of humanity, which provides for the universal peace and happiness of the world, just as our benevolent Parent intended. ...began their courses; whether the vernal <note>Spring</note> or autumnal <note>Autumn</note> equinox <note>The two times of year when day and night are of equal length.</note> has been disputed; but I cannot think with any true effect <note>meaning: no definitive conclusion has been reached by scholars.</note>.
    To come at a solution of this question; in my judgment, we must chiefly apply to the vegetable world <note>the kingdom of plants</note>, which is to the nature and reason of things. For we may be assured, the supreme artificer <note>God, viewed as the Great Architect or Master Craftsman of the universe.</note> acted with the greatest reason and judgment. We are to consider the situation of paradise, where our first parents were made, where they first lived. As to the time of the year, neither the Jewish practice, nor the sacred memoirs <note>the Bible or holy scriptures</note> do further us much. The Jews had two commencements <note>start dates</note> of the year: the vernal equinox, which they called sacred, as by it all their religious festivals were regulated; and that of the autumnal equinox, which was the civil head of the year, for bargains and agriculture.
    If religion be the primary intent of our living, to provide for a more permanent state future; if the sabbatical duty be the first and most important business of life here, the sacred year is infinitely preferable: in the same degree as things eternal excel the finite. This would answer our question at once. But let us consult nature, the law of creation.
    Survey the field, the garden, the woods, at autumn. Take along with us this preliminary consideration: that the sacred history was wrote in a southern country; our first parents lived about the influx <note>the mouth, where the river flows into the sea</note> of the Euphrates into the Persian gulf; which is many degrees of latitude warmer than us.

<insert>Such</insert> are over. The beautiful season of spring; the flourishing and fruitful view of rich summer; the corn, the wine, the olive are inned <note>gathered in from the fields</note>: the face of the earth universally robbed of its ornamenting attire; appears squalid, bare and gray: the forest leaves sickening, and yellow, ready to drop. All this is worse in the latitude of paradise <note>Eden was traditionally thought to be in a warm, fertile climate</note>, than in our own country, or in that of *Judea*.

Can this be thought the time of the world’s birthday? With equal reason may we suppose our first mother <note>Eve</note> was delivered into the arms of Adam, not in the prime of youth <note>i.e., that she was created already aged</note>.

Did it become the author of all beauty and elegance, to exhibit his new-made world, in a decaying and decrepit state?

Where is *the grass and the herb, whose seed was in itself, the like of the fruit tree?* <note>A paraphrase of Genesis 1:11-12</note> This indicates the spring: the seed in the plants, the fruit yet unproduced.

A word or two on the animal creation, will confirm the sentiment.

1. For generation <note>reproduction and birth</note> of animals, performed generally in spring, or summer.

2. For such as lay up their summer provision, for winter maintenance. The florist bee, the farmer ant, must necessarily perish, before the winter is over, if produced in autumn. The universal herd of the insect, the fly kind, die then, and prepare for their tomb <note>referring to the end of their life cycle or winter dormancy</note>.

3. Those animals that sleep through the winter, have all the fine season of the year to fatten themselves, and prepare for their torpid state <note>hibernation</note>; without which provision, they could not survive, till spring.

Such are snakes, and their kind; toads, tortoises, snails, dormice, all reptiles, spiders, butterflies, and flies in general. <note>The author is listing animals that hibernate or emerge in spring, continuing his point that a spring creation allows these creatures to begin their life cycles naturally.</note>

On the other hand, who does not see the fitness and the propriety of the joyful season of spring—of the vernal equinox <note>The time in March when day and night are of equal length, marking the start of spring</note>, the world’s birthday—for the reviviscence <note>The act of coming back to life or reawakening</note> of these animals, and for the generation of others? Then it is that all nature smiles, like a virgin birth just come from the hand of the divine artificer <note>A common 18th-century term for God as a master craftsman or architect</note>, bright and charming.

Then, it was as when the angelic choir shouted for joy at the stupendous scene of elegance and beauty! <note>A reference to the Book of Job 38:7, describing the angels' reaction to the creation of the world.</note>

It is not so in the autumnal season. But now let us survey the green fields. First, the yellow *Chelidonium* or pilewort <note>original: "Chelidonium pilewort"; likely the Lesser Celandine, one of the first flowers of spring</note>, which proclaims the joyful approach of the sun. Next, the meadow is bespangled with white daisies. Then they change their livery <note>A term for a distinctive uniform; here, the changing colors of the field</note> to the burnished gold of buttercups <note>original: "field-ranunculus"</note> and dandelions. The flowers of the latter, by a wonderful mechanism, change their flat surface into an inimitable globular head, such as is faintly represented in the frontispiece engraving <note>The illustration at the very beginning of the book</note>.

This, along with Jupiter’s Beard <note>original: "barba Jovis"</note> and the like, is a most noble picture in miniature of the great world—a starry orb around us in a clear night. We may well suppose each star to be placed in the hexangular <note>Six-sided or hexagonal</note> manner of the seeds of this plant, if we could view them from the outside of their vast circumference.

We must suppose, that this immense plain of suns, and systems of worlds, the milky way, is infinitely extended on all sides, *in every direction.* <note>original: "quaquaversum"; a Latin term meaning in every direction or towards every side.</note> so that it divides infinite space, into two great halves; like a fair and beautiful garden, well planted and adorned, in the middle of an extended desart. <note>The author uses "desart" in the older sense of a wild, uninhabited, or empty space.</note>

Thus we are to understand the work of Creation in general; respecting us particularly, as receited by MOSES.

human mind; and the little pictures we have given from the vegetable world <note>The author refers to the botanical illustrations on previous pages, using them as metaphors for the vastness of the cosmos.</note> help us to form an idea of that supreme Being, who built so large, so deep, so high! How earnestly do we seek to be in the favor of this great Being!

Wherever we can pry into the works of nature with additional eyes <note>A reference to using telescopes or microscopes to see beyond human sight.</note>, nothing appears but amazing art, contrivance, and curiosity—though even in a very minute part, the most extended thought of any created Being cannot comprehend the whole. And all of this was made by one *command* <note>original: "Fiat." A Latin term meaning "let it be done," referring to God's spoken command during the creation of the world in the Book of Genesis.</note> of the all-creating <note>original: "omnific"</note> Word.

After a little contemplation of this stupendous scene, let us return to reflect again on the time of its building, when a point was fixed in infinite time, and a boundary began in infinite space: when the *garden-plot* of worlds was planned and planted. **GOD** almighty, speaking in human terms, surveyed it with delight, seeing it was truly *good*; thus providing for an immense family throughout endless ages. We are lost in the unfathomable depth of his kindness!

We cannot possibly judge it otherwise than that the spring was the fittest time to celebrate the world’s great birthday. And can we think that **GOD**, the author of all harmony, elegance, and beauty, does not always act in the way that is most fit? It is that season which ushers in all the luster of the earthly flowers; the first efforts of teeming nature after a winter’s captivity. Must it be deprived of the honor of nature’s newness <note>original: "virginity"</note>, just sprung from the void and formless depth of *the primordial void*? <note>original: "Chaos." In this context, it refers to the state of the universe before God brought order to it.</note>

<meta>This page continues the author’s argument that the world was created in the spring. Now the violet, primrose, cowslip, the hyacinth, daffodil, narcissus, and a thousand more beauteous, fragrant, and shining forms of inimitable painting, excite the admiration, the love, the complacence, and every tender passion of the human mind; these lift up our hearts to join in the chorus of the aerial harbingers <note>Likely referring to songbirds that signal the arrival of spring.</note> and salute the rising and resplendent ornaments of the <term>vernal equinox</term> <note>The time in March when day and night are of equal length, marking the start of spring.</note>.

These offer themselves to the female beauties of the rational world to make <term>chaplets</term> <note>Wreaths or circular head-coverings made of flowers.</note>, garlands, and <term>festoons</term> <note>Decorative chains or strings of flowers hung in curves.</note> of intermixed textures, colors, and odors; to celebrate nuptials and festivities; to adorn our churches, altars, and our state rooms; to fill our urns and flower pots, as if to imitate the great world outside in miniature.

In the spring, the dews of heaven are shed plenteously; the equinoctial springs arise, along with gentle showers and mild vernal airs. All of these conspire to welcome the happy season and crown the earth with fatness <note>A biblical expression meaning "abundance" or "fertility."</note>. *In wisdom hast thou made them all, the earth is full of thy riches, O Lord.*

*Blessed of the Lord is his hand, for the precious things of heaven, for the dew, and for the deep that coucheth beneath.* By this "deep" is understood the <term>abyss</term> <note>An old geological theory suggesting a vast reservoir of fresh water existed in the center of the earth.</note> in the center of the earth, the receptacle of fresh water, strained through the rock under the ocean and from there drawn up through proper veins for springs and fountains by the attractive power of the sun and moon, just as they create the tides of the ocean. This is the long-sought-for secret of the origin of springs and fountains. *For he hath founded the earth upon the seas, and prepared it on the floods.* <note>The author uses these biblical verses to support his scientific theory that gravity and lunar attraction pull fresh water from the earth's core to the surface.</note>

And this piece of philosophy **MOSES** was sensible of, in the last quoted passage. *Deuteronomy* 33:13–14. <note>The author refers to the "Blessing of Moses," where Moses blesses the tribes of Israel before his death, specifically describing the natural bounty of the land of Joseph.</note>

In spring, the medicinal herbs come forth in plenty, as well as the <term>esculent</term> <note>Edible; fit to be eaten as food.</note> plants for food, for the animals and for man. Then it is that he cuts the grass and herbage for winter store for his cattle, to make up the deficiencies of decaying nature; this is a <term>pregnant argument</term> <note>A compelling or highly significant argument.</note> against an autumnal creation.

For who can deny that autumn is a state of decay—the languishing, decrepit year? With days shortening, winds murmuring, and all the world wearing a melancholy aspect, it is quite unworthy of a new creation.

Can we, with any just reason, think the luxuriant beauties of the summer are to be postponed for another year, or that they should be barren and banished from the blissful seat of the Garden of Eden? We grieve to think that *Eve*, our mother, should see no roses and lilies to deck her bridal bed, and lack all the gay assemblage fit to adorn the happy plains—the seat of her innocence and recent charms.

In such a matter, philosophy and the reason and nature of things are to be our guide, not chronological calculations; these may be founded on an erroneous basis, but certainly cannot have reason for their support. Must we suppose that providence acts in an irrational method?

<summary>The author argues that a spring creation is more logical and divinely fitting than an autumnal one, citing the biblical abundance described by Moses and the aesthetic necessity of flowers in the Garden of Eden.</summary>

Thus we find in the <term>Mosaic economy</term> <note>The religious, social, and agricultural system established by the laws of Moses in the Old Testament.</note>, there were four harvests in the year. 1. the hay harvest, as with us. 2. the barley harvest, when an *offering* of a first sheaf was made in the temple. This is the <term>vernal equinox</term> <note>The time in spring (around March 20) when day and night are of equal length.</note>, our *Easter*-time. 3. the wheat harvest fifty days after. This is our <term>Whitsun</term> <note>A Christian festival, also known as Pentecost, occurring seven weeks after Easter.</note> festival. 4. the harvest of grapes and olives. This last was the <term>autumnal equinox</term> <note>The time in autumn (around September 22) when day and night are of equal length.</note>. Is it likely then, is it consonant to common reason, that GOD should create the world, as it were, out of all season, beyond all seasons? Beside, we are to take into the account, that the scene of paradise was still more southerly than *Judea*.

But let us remember, after every one of these harvests, they kept a solemn and religious feast, in honor of, and in gratitude to, the bounteous giver of all their blessings. This was called, rejoicing before the Lord.

> They sang, *Psalm* CXLV. 10. <note>Psalm 145:10–11.</note> *All thy works praise thee O Lord, and thy saints give thanks unto thee.*
> *They speak of the glory of thy kingdom, and talk of thy power.*

Such were the grateful sentiments of our pious founder, in his serious hours, when he stood with silent admiration in the midst of his garden, contemplating the glories of the <term>vegetable kingdom</term> <note>A traditional term for the world of plants, used here to emphasize its divine organization and majesty.</note>.

by art; by the wisest of mortals! and this manner of expression pleads strongly, for creation being at the <term>vernal equinox</term><note>The spring equinox, occurring around March 21st; the author suggests that the world's creation was perfectly timed with the season of rebirth.</note>.

What can be more entertaining, more wonderful, than to contemplate the particular natures of plants: and that but in a general, and diffusive <note>Wide-ranging or extensive.</note> view?

We cannot but observe, how plants are made, and contrived for their common situation; whether by land or sea, for fresh, or salt water, for standing, or running stream, shade or sunshine, hills or valleys. How the **water lily** allows itself a long stem, proportionate to its depth of water, where it abides; like as sailors give their anchors more or less rope, answerable to distance from the bottom. There the flower lies secure on the surface; basks itself in the sun by day, expands its foliage, and delicate white flower: contracting itself against nocturnal dew.

Hence the Egyptians symbolized creation by the *lotus*, a similar water plant: a human figure in a resting posture, couching upon it. The flower growing on the water, means, that GOD created all things from there, according to the doctrine of *Moses* <note>Referring to the biblical account in Genesis where the Spirit of God moved upon the face of the waters.</note>. The figure sitting on it, points out, GOD resting from all his labors. He holds a whip in one hand, to drive off all evil powers. He puts the finger of his other hand upon his lips, as creating all things, *by the word of his mouth* <note>The author is describing the iconography of Harpocrates, the Greco-Roman god of silence and secrets, adapted from the Egyptian child-god Horus.</note>.

Other plants by land are formed for paths, and the wayside: where they must be trampled on. Such is *knot-grass* <note>A hardy plant (*Polygonum aviculare*) known for its resistance to being stepped on.</note>, with a small, thick and hard, woody leaf; and a minute flower. <term>Ruellius's crowfoot</term><note>original: *coronopus Ruellii*. A plant now known as swine-cress, named in honor of the 16th-century botanist Jean Ruel.</note> is similar, a pretty imitation of a stag's horn, also called **swine-cress**.

--- Page 76 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s reflections on the "vegetable kingdom," transitioning from the symbolism of the lotus to the specific biological habits and movements of various plants, which the author views as evidence of divine design.</meta>

Some plants throw out the flowers before the leaves, such as **coltsfoot**; some trees do the same, such as **apricots**. Some give the fruit first, such as the **fig**.

Creepers and climbing plants are furnished with tendrils—arms, hands, and fingers—to seize and hold fast whatever is nearest to them. The various ways in which they do this, and the different forms of their tendrils, are curious and to be admired: the **ivy**, **vine**, **Virginia creepers**, **briony**, **hops**, <term>viorna</term><note>An old name for *Clematis vitalba*, also known as "Traveller's Joy," a climbing plant common in English hedgerows.</note>, and the like. How beautifully does this latter adorn the <term>quickset hedges</term><note>A hedge made of living plants, usually hawthorn, planted closely together to form a barrier.</note>, and delight the traveler with its curious flower and woolly tufts?

Kidney beans, besides their admirable faculty of twisting themselves around their supports, have the stalks of their branches at an acute angle below, making it easier to catch hold of anything. Most of these spiral plants twist as the sun goes, from east to west; some, for variety, go the contrary way.

Some plants deserve our admiration not only for their variegated flowers, but for producing their various flowers on the same root, such as the <term>marvel of Peru</term><note>The *Mirabilis jalapa*, a tropical plant famous for having flowers of different colors (like yellow, pink, and white) growing on the same individual plant.</note>. Similarly, the **Russian poppies** might employ ten painters for ten mornings together, using all their skill and colors to copy the inexhaustible variety of their <term>fugacious</term><note>Short-lived or quickly fading; used here to describe the brief bloom of a poppy.</note> tints.

Most plants salute the rising sun and expand their several forms to adorn and perfume the coming day, carrying on their spicy wings our morning <term>ejaculations</term><note>In this historical context, "ejaculations" refers to short, sudden, and fervent prayers or expressions of devotion.</note> as incense to the King of Heaven. At night, they crowd themselves together and compose themselves for sleep. So *Anagoras*<note>Likely referring to the Greek philosopher Anaxagoras, who was known for his early scientific theories about the natural world, including the idea that plants possess a form of sensation or "soul."</note> the philosopher held that plants slept, admonishing us of the time to take the balmy gift of repose.

But evening primrose, and all the *Lysimachias* <term>Lysimachia</term><note>Commonly known as Loosestrife, these yellow or white flowers were historically used in herbal medicine.</note>, *convolvulus* <term>convolvulus</term><note>A genus of plants including the morning glory and bindweed, known for their trumpet-shaped flowers.</note>, passion tree, and others, put forth their flowers in the evening, to grace the silence of the summer night. Gum *cistus* <term>gum cistus</term><note>Also called the Rock Rose (*Cistus ladanifer*); its blossoms are famously short-lived, often falling off just hours after opening.</note> opens its excellent flower very early in the morning, goes off in the afternoon; when the evening primrose succeeds. And so of many more.

How do we behold the majesty of the sunflower, emulous of the golden orb of the sun, to which it so assiduously turns!

The palaces of princes—Babylonian, Egyptian, Cretan, called Labyrinths—the Persepolis, and the like, have not so well merited descriptions as have the *gardens* of the ancients. How celebrated those of *Alcinous* <note>In Homer's *Odyssey*, King Alcinous possessed a magnificent, mythical garden that bore fruit all year round.</note>, on which *Homer* bestows so many lines. *Alexander* <note>Alexander the Great.</note> received his ambassadors sent from all quarters of the globe in a garden; and there gave audience. Famous were the Babylonian gardens, and those of *Lucullus* <note>Lucius Licinius Lucullus (c. 118–57 BC), a Roman general famous for the extravagant gardens he established in Rome.</note> at Rome. The scent, as well as sight, is entertained from the flowery world.

Gold and gems give way to vegetable beauty. The greatest pomp of a Victor’s triumph was not complete without the laurel garland, without the palm. The oak furnished the most honorable, the civic crown <note>The *Corona civica*, a wreath of oak leaves given to a Roman soldier who saved the life of a fellow citizen.</note>, for saving the life of citizens. The peaceful olive was brought to *Noah* <note>original: "NOA"</note> by a dove; to denote that God’s anger was assuaged.

So the gods of antiquity had peculiar plants dedicated to them: the oak to *Jupiter*, myrtle to *Venus*, ivy to *Bacchus*, poplar to *Hercules*, and the like. Such were the honors paid to plants.

<meta>This page continues the author’s philosophical and spiritual exploration of the "vegetable kingdom." While the previous page ended with a mention of "Gold," this page shifts to the moral and intellectual value of gardens, linking botanical appreciation to biblical virtue and the character of humanity.</meta>

We judge the character and intellect of mankind by their affection for a garden; the site of love and pleasure, of study and contemplation, of philosophy, and of serenity; even the rapturous thought of divine truths that lift the mind to heaven.

The world of plants may be called the chief happiness of life; a treasury of pleasure without pain or unease. It provides a lasting delight that is always new, without causing discontent or vexation.

Balaam <note>A prophet mentioned in the Book of Numbers who was summoned to curse the Israelites but blessed them instead.</note>, who was in truth a Druid <note>The author here makes a characteristic 18th-century connection between biblical figures and the ancient Celtic priestly class known as Druids, reflecting the "Celtomania" of the era.</note>, could not find a better comparison for the grand and admirable arrangement of the Israelite camp in the wilderness than to a garden. *Numbers 24*.

> *How goodly are your tents, O Jacob, and your tabernacles, O Israel! They are spread out like valleys, like gardens by the river’s side; like the trees of lign-aloes <term>lign-aloes</term><note>An aromatic wood mentioned in the Bible, often identified with agarwood, highly valued for its fragrance and used in incense.</note> which the Lord has planted: like cedar trees beside the waters!*

The Psalmist <note>The traditional author of the Book of Psalms, usually identified as King David.</note> thus describes the plenty and prosperity possessed by a good man: *The trees of the Lord are full of sap; even as the cedars of Lebanon <note>original: "Libanus." These massive trees were a biblical symbol of strength, longevity, and divine favor.</note>, which the Lord has planted.*

And when he would sum up the height of blessedness and human happiness, he says: *He shall be like a tree planted by the rivers of water, that brings forth its fruit in due season; its leaf shall not wither, and whatever he does shall prosper.*

And thus shall the man be blessed who fears the Lord.

From these concepts of the plant world is derived the very picture of heaven itself, as described in *Revelation 22 <note>original: "Apocalypse XXII," the Latin-derived name for the final book of the New Testament.</note>.*

The Druids, came from the East, in the early ages of the world ; from ABRAHAM they learnt the use of sacred groves. <note>The author is referencing a popular 18th-century theory—championed by antiquarians like William Stukeley—that the Druids were "Patriarchal Christians" who emigrated from the Near East and preserved the religion of the biblical patriarchs like Abraham.</note> they were of the first, and patriarchal religion. they held the eternal geniture <note>Meaning "generation" or "begetting."</note> of the Son of God, whom they called *Mithras*, meaning the mediatorial deity. <note>Mithras was a Persian deity, but in the 1700s, scholars often conflated various ancient "sun gods" or "mediators" with the Christian concept of the Logos or Son of God.</note>

*Chyndonax* was an Archdruid, whose sepulchral urn was found at Dijon in the year 1598. upon it this inscription :

->In the grove of Mithras, this burial mound<-
->covers the body<-
->of Chyndonax, the Chief Priest.<-
->Keep away, you unholy ones; the guardian spirits watch over my ashes.<-
<note>original Greek: "ΜΙΘΡΗΣ ΕΝ ΟΡΓΑΔ ΚΩΜΑ ΤΟ ϹΩΜΑ ΚΑΛΥΠΤΕΙ ΧΥΝΔΟΝΑΚΤΟΣ ΙΕΡΕΩϹ ΑΡΧΗΓΟΥ ΔΥϹΕΒ ΑΠΕΧΟΥ. ΛΥϹΙΟΙ ΚΟΝ ΟΡΩϹΙ."
