## IV.
# C O S M O G O N Y,
<note>original: "Cosmogonia"; the branch of science or philosophy dealing with the origin of the universe.</note>
->OR<-
### Spring, asserted to be the Time of Creation.

> *Spring was eternal. The gentle Zephyrs <note>The personified west wind, considered the herald of spring.</note>, with their warm breezes, caressed the flowers that grew without seeds.*
<note>original: "Ver erat æternum. placidique, tepentibus auris, Mulcebant Zephyri, natos sine semine flores." — Ovid, *Metamorphoses*, Book 1, lines 107–108.</note>
->Ovid.<-

As I have, in these discourses, given a hint concerning the *epoch* <term>epoch: the specific point in time used as the beginning of an era; here referring to the exact date and season of the world's beginning.</term> of Creation; a question wherein the *honor* of the vegetable world is so much concerned, which I had undertaken to illustrate: I judge it not a thing impertinent to our subject, nor unacceptable to the reader, to enlarge upon it, from a work I wrote thirty years ago; *The Rule of Mosaic Chronology* <note>original: "Mosaicæ Chronologiæ Canon"; refers to the author's earlier work on the timeline of the Bible as recorded by Moses.</note>: mentioned in the preface to *Abury* <note>Referring to "Abury, a Temple of the British Druids" (1743), a famous work by the antiquarian William Stukeley regarding the stone circles at Avebury.</note>.

<meta>This page continues the author's investigation into the exact season of the world's creation. of all the parts, though the head, the *root* <note>original: "radix"; here used to mean the starting point or foundation of a chronological system.</note> of time, between us, be a little distant.

The dispute is, whether the world was created in a state of bloom, and beauty, or in a state of decay; in other terms, at the spring <note>original: "vernal"</note>, or autumn <note>original: "autumnal"</note> equinox. It is agreed on all hands, to pitch on an equinox <term>equinox: the two times a year when day and night are of equal length, marking the start of spring or autumn.</term>, for the great work.

I shall not dwell on the argument taken from reason, whether as to the vegetable, or the animal world; to avoid wordiness <note>original: "prolixity"</note>, which on this head might be extended to an unmeasurable length: the hints I have given, every one, that thinks upon it, can add to, abundantly, out of their own mind.

It is easy to say, astronomical calculation must settle the point. It is allowed, it may help, if it is truly founded. But I apprehend, it does not impeach our assumption. The circle of time is the same in itself, whether commencing at the spring, or autumn equinox: as a plain circle described by a pair of compasses, is the same, whether you begin it, at A or at B. What I have further to say, I shall comprise with brevity, under these considerations.

1. Recite the authorities of the learned, ancient and modern.

2. The customs of ancient nations.

3. An astronomical character from the ecliptic <term>ecliptic: the circular path the Sun appears to follow across the sky over the course of a year.</term>.

4. An argument deducible from the fossil bodies of the Great Flood <note>original: "the Deluge"; referring to the biblical flood of Noah.</note>, in general.

5. From the customs of the Druids <note>Ancient Celtic priests and scholars in Britain and Gaul, whom the author believes preserved ancient traditions about the world's origin.</note>.

6. From some particular fossil remains <note>original: "foſſil bodys"</note>, and ancient coins.

7. From the reason and nature of things, which I have done already in my preceding discourses on vegetables; and much may be added, in regard to the animal kingdom.

The generation of beasts, birds, fishes; the innumerable tribes of insects and reptiles, is performed in spring: in general, the whole animal world. And the divine benediction, *increase and multiply* <note>Referring to the command given by God in Genesis 1:22 and 1:28.</note>, was to take place immediately; it is strangely absurd to postpone it until next year, if in the mean time, all animals were to lead an inactive, uncomfortable life, without the natural tenderness of taking care of their offspring. This, if we consider it, is an absurdity by no means to be overcome.

8. An argument is deducible from the time of the Deluge <term>Deluge: the great flood described in the Book of Genesis.</term>.

But first it is necessary to remark upon the insufficiency of concluding the *phase* <note>original: "phaſis"; refers to the appearance or stage of the moon's cycle.</note> of the moon in opposition to the sun, or the appearance of a full moon, at creation; suggested to be gathered from the words of *Moses*.

*Genesis* I. 14. GOD *made the sun and moon, for signs, and for seasons, for days and years.* This means nothing more than the general use of the sun and moon, in common to the whole world: even as much as the subsequent institution of the sabbath, which regards every nation under heaven; not the Jews, only.

But especially, nothing can be gathered from it to prove the moon was created at full; nor is a full moon a necessary requirement of Creation. For it seems as improper that the moon should first be seen in a state of diminution as for the world to be created in a state of decay, when the days shorten, the rains, springs, and tides rise, cold dews, frosty evenings, and blustering winds occur, and birds of passage are thinking of their return. In a word, as to plants and animals, it is a total reversal <note>original: "renversment"</note> of the order of nature before it has even begun, and every consideration opposes it.

My learned friend Mr. *Kennedy* <note>John Kennedy (1698–1782), a rector and chronologist who published "A New Method of Stating and Explaining the Scripture Chronology" in 1751.</note>, the chronologer (p. 223), accommodates the *Jewish* festivals to creation, which he calls *Jewish* epochs, as if the time of the patriarchal religion <note>The religious practice of the biblical patriarchs (like Noah and Abraham) before the formal laws were given to Moses.</note> was of no account.

The Bible was for nobler purposes than to give us technical rules of astronomy. Nor can I persuade myself that the words "let them be for seasons" <note>original Hebrew: *haju lemoadim* (היו למועדים), from Genesis 1:14.</note> mean that the sun and moon were appointed only for the regulation of *Jewish* festivals.

My friend judges that the autumnal equinox <term>Equinox: the two times each year when day and night are of equal length; the autumnal equinox marks the start of autumn.</term> at creation was twenty-four hours after a full moon, on the third day (p. 221). Now *ADAM* was made on the sixth, when the moon was in its waning phase <note>original: "wain"</note>; this might make him fear it was going to vanish, instead of marking out a day of festivity.

We are not to draw our conclusions from the *Jews*; the world was not made for them any more than for any other nation. But even *they* began their ecclesiastical year from the vernal equinox <note>The spring equinox, signifying the start of the religious calendar.</note>, and their civil year with the new moon, not the full—and that is their practice to this day. So they begin their months, and so did most

ancient nations. But the Cosmogony <term>Cosmogony: A theory or account of the origin and development of the universe.</term> is a general description of the creation, not a particular one. All the Greeks and Romans began their new years and months with the new moon; it is natural. None ever began them with a full moon; it is unnatural. The custom would necessarily flow from the original and primeval institution—from creation itself. And a chronological calculation from any other point must necessarily be erroneous. Calculations in this case cannot be like those for eclipses and such *phenomena* <note>original: "phænomena"</note>, for the times of such are stated and fixed in nature, from which the basis of the calculation is drawn. But to investigate the time of creation by calculations is to apply an immeasurable rule of measure from nature before nature was even made.

The latter is more eligible because of its increasing light, not waning.

is distinguished in sacred history by a greater variety of remarkable events than the autumnal equinox.

2. It is rational to suppose that these events happened at the beginning of the original, patriarchal year. Such events include the calling of ABRAHAM; his covenant with *GOD* in the *Shechinah* <term>Shechinah: A Hebrew term for the dwelling or settling of the divine presence of God, often depicted as a cloud or light.</term>, as that vision ought to be understood; the *Exodus* [of the Israelites from Egypt], and many others recited there. Doubtless, the original records of creation, and of the world before and after the Great Flood <note>original: "antediluvian and postdiluvian world"</note>, were in the custody of ABRAHAM and transmitted through his family to *Moses*. Since the affairs of ABRAHAM are admittedly reckoned by years beginning at the vernal equinox <term>Vernal equinox: The moment in spring, around March 21, when day and night are of equal length, marking the start of the astronomical year.</term>, how can we doubt that this was the result of the original chronology?

The *Mosaic* dispensation <note>The religious system and laws given to the Israelites through Moses.</note> eventually came to an end; at last, the patriarchal year was revived at the Annunciation to the blessed Virgin Mary at the vernal equinox—that is, the incarnation of our Savior. Therefore, his birth surely occurred at the winter solstice; finally, at the vernal equinox, *Christ* accomplished our redemption <note>A reference to the Crucifixion and Resurrection occurring in the spring.</note>.

Of this original commencement of the year, we find many remnants in antiquity. The *Persian* *Nowruz* <note>original: "Neurouz"</note> is the name of New Year’s Day, which occurs in the spring.

The *Tyrian* and *Damascene* years began in spring (*Noris, Epochs, p. 382* <note>Refers to Enrico Noris’s "Annus et Epochae Syromacedonum," a 17th-century work on ancient calendars.</note>). The same is true for the city of *Sychar*, where ABRAHAM lived, which was later called *Neapolis*—no doubt following the custom of the great Patriarch. The *Antiochians* did the same, which is why they stamped a ram <note>The ram represents Aries, the first sign of the zodiac, which begins at the spring equinox.</note> on their coins (*Vaillant, Colonies, Vol. II, p. 321* <note>Refers to Jean Foy-Vaillant’s work on the coinage of Roman colonies.</note>). So too did the ancient *Arabians* (*Noris, Epochs, p. 91*). From these peoples came our British ancestors, and thus—

our Druids did the same. From the same source, they reckoned time by nights <note>Following the ancient custom of starting the 24-hour day at sunset rather than sunrise</note>. *Simplicius's commentary on Aristotle's Physics, Book 5.* The Egyptians did so at first; the early Roman year was the same, according to *Sir Isaac Newton’s Chronology*, page 78.

The *Syrians*, *Assyrians*, and *Chaldeans* did the like. This shows it to be the Eastern, patriarchal rule.

*Macrobius, Book 1, chapter 21*, argues that the "birthday" of the world began with the sun in *Aries* <note>The ram, the first sign of the zodiac, which the sun enters at the spring equinox</note>. He repeats this again elsewhere. Hence, all the ancient astronomers begin their works with *Aries*.

*Syncellus* the chronicler writes that creation, or the first *Nisan* <note>The first month of the Hebrew calendar, occurring in March–April</note>, began at the vernal equinox.

All the years in *Ptolemy’s excellent Canon* begin at the vernal equinox: the general *computus* <term>computus: The science of calculating time and the calendar, specifically used by the church to determine the dates of festivals.</term> of the East and of antiquity.

<meta>This page continues the author's exploration of ancient calendars. Here, he argues that the specific religious laws given to the Jews were intentionally designed by God to contrast with the older "patriarchal" religion—which the author links to the British Druids—to prevent the influence of idolatry.</meta>

<sig>K 2</sig>

I believe that, in the institution of the **Jewish economy** <note>The religious system and administration of laws given to the Israelites under Moses.</note>, God ordered many particulars in direct opposition to the **patriarchal religion** <note>The original form of worship practiced by the early biblical fathers, such as Abraham, before the Mosaic law was established.</note>; specifically where those earlier practices had been perverted for the purposes of idol worship. To provide a few instances: He established a covered temple instead of the open-air ones, and a square temple instead of a round one. For this reason, the Israelites were frequently commanded, upon entering the land of Canaan, to destroy their altars and beat down their **pillars** <note>Standing stones or monuments marking sacred sites.</note>. By these pillars, we understand their first and patriarchal temples, such as those built by **Abraham** himself. They were also to cut down their **groves** <note>Groups of trees planted for religious worship, often associated with ancient pagan or patriarchal rites.</note>, such as were planted by **Abraham** himself, because all these had been desecrated by the Canaanites. Similarly, the Sunday **Sabbath** <note>The author suggests that the original day of rest was Sunday, which was changed to Saturday during the Exodus from Egypt to distinguish the Jews from other peoples, then restored to Sunday in the Christian era.</note> of the patriarchs was changed at the Exodus for Saturday, and then restored in Christianity.

We have innumerable temples of this same primitive form in the British islands; these are the works of our **Druids** <note>Ancient Celtic priests and scholars. The author, likely William Stukeley, believed the Druids practiced a pure form of the "patriarchal" religion brought from the Near East.</note>, who came from the country of Canaan before idolatry became prevalent there. They used similar groves, where they celebrated the solemnities of the **autumnal equinox** <note>The time in September when day and night are of equal length, marking the start of autumn.</note>.

With a similar purpose of creating a distinction, the **patriarchs' moon of the sixth day** <note>The moon as it appears six days after it is new, used by the ancient fathers to mark the start of their months.</note> was changed into the Jewish full moon for the commencement of their festivities.

**Archbishop Usher** <note>James Ussher (1581–1656), the Archbishop of Armagh, famous for his chronology that dated the creation of the world to 4004 BC.</note> makes the sixth day of the moon fall upon his fourth day of creation, Wednesday, October 26, in the **year 710 of the Julian Period** <note>original: A P J. 710; this chronological system places year 710 at 4004 BC.</note>. This is half a year after my own assignment of the date <note>The author argues for a creation in the spring, whereas Ussher famously calculated an autumn creation.</note>.

I understand that, in the institution of the **Jewish economy** <term>The religious and social system of the ancient Israelites as established by the laws of Moses.</term>, God ordered many specific details in direct opposition to the **primitive and patriarchal religion** <note>The original, "pure" religion which the author believes was practiced by figures like Noah and Abraham before the Mosaic law was given.</note>; particularly in cases where those earlier practices had been corrupted for the purpose of idol worship. To provide a few instances: a roofed temple was used instead of an open-air one, and a square temple instead of a circular one. This explains why the Israelites were so often commanded, upon entering the land of **Canaan** <note>The "Promised Land" of the Bible, located in the ancient Levant.</note>, *to destroy their altars and beat down their pillars*. By these "pillars," we are to understand their earliest patriarchal temples, such as those built by Abraham himself. They were also *to cut down their sacred groves*, similar to those planted by Abraham himself, because all these sites had been desecrated by the Canaanites. Similarly, the Sunday Sabbath observed by the patriarchs was changed at the time of the **Exodus** <note>The departure of the Israelites from Egypt under Moses.</note> to Saturday, and was later restored to Sunday in **Christianity**.

We have countless temples of this same ancient form in the British Islands—the work of our **Druids** <note>Ancient Celtic priests whom the author identifies as followers of the original patriarchal faith.</note>. These people came from the land of Canaan before idolatry became widespread there. They also used similar groves, where they celebrated the solemn rituals of the **autumnal equinox** <note>The time in September when day and night are of equal length, marking the start of autumn.</note>.

For a similar purpose of creating distinction, the patriarchs' custom of starting the month on the sixth day of the moon was changed into the Jewish practice of starting on the full moon for the commencement of their festivals.

Archbishop **Ussher** <note>James Ussher (1581–1656), the Anglo-Irish Archbishop famous for his chronology of the Bible.</note> calculates that the sixth day of the moon fell upon his fourth day of creation: *Wednesday, October 26*, in the **year 710 of the Julian Period** <term>Anno Periodi Julianae: A chronological system used by historians to count years from a fixed point in 4713 BC.</term> This is half a year after my own assigned date.

6. I have ever held, that the **great Deluge** <term>The biblical Great Flood survived by Noah.</term> began with the new year, as well as Creation. This was to last a whole year. We need not so much be under a concern for its commencement time; as for its termination. All time, every season was equally ruinous, to whatever was upon the face of the earth. But we are to consider the renovation of things, at its period. Can any one think, the autumn proper, for restoration of the animal, of the vegetable kingdom, after so terrible a catastrophe? Quite otherwise. For all that can be said with good reason on that head, against the autumnal Creation, holds equally conclusive, against the Flood.

But *Moses* <note>The biblical prophet credited with writing the Book of Genesis.</note> decides the controversy peremptorily <note>Meaning decisively or absolutely, leaving no room for further debate.</note>, and precisely. The Flood began on the second month, the seventeenth day of the month; which certainly was about the middle of May. At the same time next year, it ended. Then was the time for germination of plants, for generation of animals. It was a new, a second creation.

Sir *Isaac Newton* <note>The famous scientist who, later in life, wrote extensively on revising ancient history and biblical timelines.</note> shortens chronology, perhaps a little too much. I propose to carry the excellent *Ussher’s* <note>Archbishop James Ussher (1581–1656), famous for calculating the date of Creation as nightfall preceding October 23, 4004 BC.</note>, from autumnal to the **vernal equinox** <term>The start of spring (around March 21), which the author argues is the logical time for the world to have been created or reborn.</term>, upwards, and present the type thereof ensuing: and I apprehend, it has a good chance for the true. But to be certain and peremptory, I leave to the knowledge of angels.

After what I have said, which I think amounts to a sufficient demonstration; I need add no more, than an argument, equally conclusive. This I extract from my above-mentioned manuscript <note>original: "MS."</note> treatise; where I have drawn out the full calendar of the year of the Flood, in the Year of the World <term>A.M. 1656</term> <note>The "Anno Mundi" or "Year of the World" system dates events from the biblical creation; 1656 is the traditional year for the start of Noah’s Flood.</note>, completed over thirty years ago.

The Flood began in the month of May, much about the same time as creation. It was to last a full year; consequently, it was to end about the same month. This is agreeable to good sense, reason, nature, philosophy, and scripture. It was a new creation. All the same reasoning in favor of a spring creation holds here indispensably for the receding <note>original: "declension"</note> of the Flood and the renovation of nature—both of the plant <note>original: "vegetable"</note> and animal worlds.

After the earth had been soaked for a twelve-month under a mile depth of water, is it in any degree likely that autumn should be a proper time for its relief: either for drying the earth again, for the growth <note>original: "vegetation"</note> of plants, or for the breeding <note>original: "propagation"</note> of animals? The matter is so apparent, so self-evident, that I cannot persuade myself anyone can hesitate in their judgment about it. No theory or calculation can overcome these reasonings, if we admit that Almighty GOD acts with the same judgment as we would.

> NUMBERS XXII. 6. *I know <note>original: "wot," an archaic term for "know."</note>, that he whom thou blessest, is blessed; and he whom thou cursest, is cursed.*

The priesthood of the Jews was by family descent. Our Saviour, at parting with his disciples during his ascension, *lifted up his hands and blessed them.* Those who discern spiritual things know that this high prerogative remains ordinarily with the Christian priests, and extraordinarily with the bishops.

II. Let us resume the remarkable history of *Balaam*. *“Come now, I pray thee,”* says King *Balak*, *“curse me this people, for they are too mighty for me. By thy help, I may peradventure <note>perhaps</note> be able to overcome them. For I know that he whom thou blessest is blessed, and he whom thou cursest is cursed.”*

He was sufficiently aware of this divine privilege inherent in the sacred order. And *Balaam* was a Druid of eminence, an Archdruid; as we might say in modern terms, a bishop or Archbishop. <note>The author interprets the biblical prophet Balaam as a member of the Druidic order, reflecting an 18th-century antiquarian belief that Druidism was a remnant of the true patriarchal religion practiced by figures in the Old Testament.</note>

The first time the princes of *Moab* came to him, God expressly forbade him from going. The second time, when he sent a more splendid delegation <note>original: "embassage"</note>, a greater gift of money, greater promises of social advancement <note>original: "preferment"</note>, and more earnest pleading, God said to him: *“If the men come to call thee, rise up and go with them.”* What follows?

Verse <note>original: "Ver."</note> 32. he tells him, *I went out to withstand thee, because thy way is perverse before me.*

The matter, at first sight, is apparently difficult. God bade him to go, with the princes. Why then is he forbidden? Why is God’s anger kindled against him? Why is the angel sent to withstand him? How is his way perverse?

I answer: when the princes of Moab came to him the second time, God said to him, *if the men come to call thee, rise up, and go with them.*

The meaning is: let it be the business of these princes to come to thy *levee* <note>A formal morning reception of visitors, usually held by a person of high rank while rising from bed.</note>, and with humility inquire what God has said to thee this night; whether he has permitted thee to go with us?

The ambassadors of a king ought thus to behave towards the ambassadors of the King of Kings. The prophet’s *levee* is like that of going to church; not like that of going to Court for pomp and pageantry.

A parallel case we meet with in *II Kings 5: Naaman the great Syrian prince, chief General of the army of the potent king of Damascus, a great man with his master, and honorable; a mighty man of valor, he came with his horses and chariots, a vast retinue, and equipage <note>A carriage with its horses and attending servants.</note>; many talents in money, many changes of raiment, and rich presents, according to the eastern mode.* When he was to approach a prophet, a man of God, one endued with the Holy Spirit and supernatural power, he justly thought he must make a splendid figure, not appear before God with an empty hand, in an undress <note>In the 18th century, "undress" referred to informal or everyday clothing rather than ceremonial or "full dress" attire.</note>; for in all antiquity, they dressed themselves in their best

<summary>The author explains that God's anger toward Balaam stemmed from a lack of priestly humility; he argues that spiritual leaders should be approached with religious reverence rather than worldly pageantry, citing the story of Naaman as a parallel.</summary>

Let us consider the nature of this great, and superlative gift. God almighty, the supreme governor of the world, because he is infinitely above us, absolutely invisible to mortal eye, has been so extremely condescending, as to ordain a visible priesthood; men like ourselves, in nature, to whom he has delegated this extraordinary power.

A priest is his representative, to communicate a notification of his *blessings*, of his displeasure, as *Nathan* <note>A prophet and advisor to King David, famous for rebuking the king for his relationship with Bathsheba.</note> to king *David*: to deliver his rewards, and his threatenings; GOD has assured us, he will ratify it. that is <note>original: "i. e." The priest is the mediator between God and his people. he presents their offerings, their prayers, their sacrifices; and declares what is the pleasure of the Almighty.

Great was the honor, respect and reverence paid to these mediators; called men of GOD, divine persons. when *Obadiah* <note>A high-ranking official in the palace of King Ahab who remained a devout follower of God.</note> a prince, *First Kings 18* <note>original: "I. Kings XVIII."</note> met *Elijah* the prophet, he fell on his face, and worshiped him, and called him my lord.

## VI.

### The Sabbath the Foundation of Government:

#### AN ARGUMENT.

---

->*And may you yourself, Phoebus, lead me to your thresholds,*<-
->*By your own hand, held in suspense by your great divine power.*<-
<note>original: "Meque ad tua limina, Phoebe, Ipse manu, multo suspensum numine, ducas." A modified quotation from Virgil, likely invoking Apollo (Phoebus) as a symbol of divine light and truth to guide the author's reasoning.</note>
->**VIRGIL.**<-

---

> **GENESIS II. 1.** *Thus the heavens and the earth were finished, and all the host of them.*
> **2.** *And on the seventh day GOD ended his work, which he had made; and he rested on the seventh day, from all his work, which he had made.*
> **3.** *And GOD blessed the seventh day, and sanctified it; because that in it, he had rested from all his work; which GOD created, and made.*

THUS was <term>Creation</term> <note>The act of bringing the universe into existence by the divine will.</note> finished, in six days. There are two reasons, why the almighty Creator was pleased to reckon <term>reckon</term> <note>To calculate, count, or categorize a series of events.</note> this great operation, by days-works. <note>The author refers to the "days of creation" as distinct units of labor used by God to organize the process of making the world.</note>

---

->St. George’s, Queen-Square, 25 Aug. 1765.<-
<note>St. George the Martyr, Queen Square, is a historic church in Bloomsbury, London. The date and location suggest this was originally a sermon or a scholarly reflection written while the author was in residence there.</note>

<summary>This page introduces a formal argument claiming the Sabbath is the essential foundation of government, beginning with an invocation of divine guidance and a recitation of the Genesis creation narrative.</summary>
<keywords>Sabbath, Government, Creation, Genesis, Virgil, Phoebus, Divine Order, 18th Century Theology</keywords>

--- Page 126 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page continues the list of "two reasons" why the Creator organized the work of creation into six days. It follows the previous page's introduction of the Sabbath as the foundation of human government and the moral world.</meta>

1. To teach us to act in an orderly and regular manner; without which, no perfect work can be accomplished. Counsel, deliberation, thought, and wisdom must be employed in every business of consequence. A hasty effect may by chance be produced for a good purpose; but then it is a matter of chance and fortune, not of <term>prudence</term> <note>Practical wisdom and foresight in conducting one's affairs.</note>.

2. There is another, a higher, a divine purpose pursued in this regular procedure of the great <term>Architect of the world</term> <note>A common 18th-century title for God, emphasizing His role as a rational and orderly designer of the universe.</note>; to define the time we are to give for the *adoration* of our Supreme Benefactor, and to give the highest sanction that possibly could be to the Sabbath, even God Almighty’s own *example*: and to teach us the infinite importance and excellence of the duty for the *good* of the world.

In speaking to these words, my intent is to enlarge on the two great Laws of the moral world annexed and conjoined to the work of Creation. I. the Law of the Sabbath. II. the Law of Matrimony. These two are <term>coeval</term> <note>Originating at the same time or age; in this case, existing as long as the world itself.</note> with creation, a necessary part of it, and the completion thereof; for on them rests the *basis* of government.

And it is now time for us to explain this. When GOD made the world *by the word of his mouth*, he made it because he was *all good*; he was goodness itself, essential goodness, whom *Plato* calls *the Good* <note>original: "to Agathon". A reference to the Greek philosopher Plato's concept of the supreme Form of the Good, which is the source of all existence and knowledge.</note>. In a word, he made the world out of the pure principle of *goodness*.

He had created those glorious and immortal spirits, the angels. He made them to be happy, and he was pleased to *enlarge* his family to a boundless degree. He expanded the *good principle* beyond all measure: he formed the race of men. Immense was the number of those who benefited from him, the daily objects of his <term>sustenance</term> <note>Support or maintenance; the act of providing what is necessary for life.</note>.

He extended his arm to create, and he opened his hand; he keeps it open for their support. So it appears that not one single species of all his creatures—whether plant or animal—has been lost since the creation. How watchful, as well as careful, is his <term>providence</term> <note>The protective care of God as the spiritual power guiding the universe.</note> to feed his family and preserve them from all the accidents that might threaten the destruction of any particular race!

## II. We are to consider the other great Law consequent to creation, and annexed to it, the Law of Matrimony.

Behold the wisdom, as well as goodness of our almighty Father. he has completed a world, a mighty work, worthy of his skill, his power, his greatness, his *goodness*: compleat in all its parts, truly admirable in the whole.

It becomes a judicious workman that has done all this, to establish proper laws, and rules, for the administration and orderly continuance of his work. and this he has done effectually.

In the first place, he has established the <term>Law of nature</term> <note>In this context, the author refers to the physical laws of science—such as gravity and planetary motion—which were seen by 18th-century thinkers as evidence of God's constant "administration" of the universe.</note>, by which the component orbs of his System continue their perpetual and regular course. the laws of matter, and motion are settled, and admit of no variation, no deviation. the Laws of the vegetable, the animal world, are unalterable; the great parts of earth and seas, of springs, and fountains, the revolutions of seasons; all answer the character GOD at first pronounc'd concerning them, *all are very good* <note>original: "valde bona." A reference to Genesis 1:31, where God surveys His finished creation.</note>.

III. In the meantime, let us contemplate the great and important consequences drawn from a due attendance of the Sabbath: to good government; to the benefit of social life; to the peace and quiet of the world; and the safety and comfort of individuals.

After GOD Almighty made the world, He made laws, as we have seen, for the government thereof: a law of religion, and a law for the multiplication of mankind in the pure bands <note>original: "bands." Here meaning the sacred ties or bonds of marriage.</note> of matrimony.

If, therefore, government is necessary for society, these laws are to be continued as our rule and guide for all ages. He knew what was best, and most conducive to our happiness, both public and private.

Religion, therefore, is the *basis* of government, and religion is the Sabbath, as enacted at the commencement of the world.

The bias of nature is strong toward earthly things, and weak toward spiritual considerations.

Religion was made for the benefit of society, besides the moral duty of gratitude and worship toward our supreme benefactor.

The Sabbath, or religion, is not only the command of GOD, but the natural instrument of making a nation potent <term>potent: powerful and influential.</term> and flourishing. What says that great genius *Cicero*, when contemplating the amazing grandeur of the Roman empire, to which it was arrived at his time?

> “We are not,” says he, “larger men, and stronger,
> “than the Germans; wiser than the Greeks; more
> “cunning than the Gauls or Carthaginians. Why
> “then, has providence favored us, to so high a degree,
> “as in a manner to make us masters of mankind? I
> “profess, I know no reason, but that we are the most
> “religious people of mankind.” <note>original: Cicero is likely being paraphrased here from his work *De Haruspicum Responsis* (On the Responses of the Soothsayers), where he argues that Romans excelled through "piety and religion."</note>

Thus reasons that most excellent and judicious person, than whom none had penetrated deeper into the thoughts of mankind, into the nature, and reason of things. And it must be allowed, that the Romans for the first seven hundred years of their state, were the justest people upon earth; beside their incomparable valor, they excelled all nations for public faith <term>public faith: the concept of *fides*, or the collective honor and reliability of a nation in its treaties and laws.</term> and virtue.

As to what *Tully* <note>Tully is the traditional English shortening of Marcus Tullius Cicero’s middle name, by which he was commonly known to scholars in the 17th and 18th centuries.</note> calls religion, it was not our Sabbath, strictly speaking. But it was what they called religion, and practiced as such. It was equivalent; it preserved the reason of the thing, the *impression* on the mind. And we must acknowledge, it had a proper effect upon them. They had not our religion, no more than our Sabbath; but if that consideration could prove anything, it proves that we ought to be much more religious than they. It strongly proves our main point, that religion is the *foundation* of government.

The Sabbath, or religion, is not only the command of G O D, but the natural instrument of making a nation potent, and flourishing. what says that great genius *Cicero* <note>Marcus Tullius Cicero (106–43 BCE), the Roman statesman and philosopher, frequently cited by Enlightenment thinkers for his views on civic virtue.</note>, when contemplating the amazing grandeur of the Roman empire; to which it was arriv’d at his time.

> “We are not, says he, larger men, and stronger,
> “than the Germans; wiser than the Greeks; more
> “cunning than the Gauls or Carthaginians. why

Thus reasons that most excellent and judicious person, than whom none had penetrated deeper into the thoughts of mankind, into the nature, and reason of things. and it must be allow’d, that the Romans for the first seven hundred years of their state, were the justest people upon earth; beside, their incomparable valor, they excelled all nations for public faith and virtue.

As to what *Tully* <note>A common historical English name for Cicero, derived from his middle name, Tullius.</note> calls religion, it was not our Sabbath, strictly speaking. but it was what they call’d religion, and practised as such. it was equivalent; it preserved the reason of the thing, the *impression* <note>The psychological or moral effect that regular religious observance has on the conscience of the citizenry.</note> on the mind. and we must acknowledge, it had a proper effect upon them. they had not our religion, no more than our sabbath; but if that consideration could prove any thing, it proves that we ought to be much more religious than they. it strongly proves our main point, that religion is the *foundation* of government.

--- Page 144 ---
<meta>Continuity: Page 101 concluded with the author arguing that the Romans, according to Cicero, owed their greatness to their religious devotion. This page continues comparing Roman piety to contemporary practice before transitioning to the biblical origin of the Sabbath.</meta>

[ 102 ]

I wish I could say that we practice our religious exercises with more diligence than the Romans did; however, in reading their history, we find the opposite to be true.

The original Sabbath <note>The "primæval Sabbath" refers to the day of rest established at the time of Creation in the Book of Genesis.</note> was indeed lost to them, but it was replaced by other practices: every morning, the altars of their *lararium* <term>lararium: a private shrine in an ancient Roman home dedicated to the *Lares*, the guardian spirits of the household</term>, or private domestic chapels, smoked with incense. This devotion continued in their numerous public sacrifices, ceremonies, and festivities of a thousand kinds upon every occasion. I could speak at great length on these, but I will only focus on the most important matter: the *effect* produced by the best understandings they had of religion.

They experienced the true effect of religion—the deep *impression* it made upon their hearts and minds. Their rulers had the wisdom to support and encourage this, and they reaped the glorious consequences of it. They understood that it was the duty of governors to care for and enforce moral laws just as much as civil laws; the two are connected and inseparable, for the influence of the former ensures the respect due to the latter.

Let us carry our inquiry even further back, moving from the Roman to the Jewish system <term>Jewish economy: the religious, social, and political structure of the ancient Israelites under Mosaic law</term>, where GOD republished this first Law of the Sabbath. He did so with an audible voice from Mount Sinai, alongside the rest of the moral laws of the Decalogue <term>Decalogue: The Ten Commandments</term>. Indeed, the Sabbath is the primary law and the foundation of all the others; it encompasses them all. Consequently, breaking this law demands a greater punishment than breaking any of the others—at least any single one—because it protects and secures the rest.

<summary>The author compares Roman domestic and public piety to his own era, arguing that religious devotion is the foundation of civil order before tracing the Sabbath's authority back to the Ten Commandments given at Mount Sinai.</summary>
<keywords>Roman religion, lararium, domestic worship, civic virtue, Jewish Law, Sabbath, Mount Sinai, Decalogue, moral law</keywords>

--- Page 145 ---
<meta>Continuity: Page 102 transitioned from the religious devotion of the Romans to the "Jewish system," where God reaffirmed the Sabbath at Mount Sinai as part of the moral law (the Decalogue).</meta>

[ 103 ]

<meta>catchword: The</meta>

There is no doubt that the commandments carry the same authority and the same sanction; the breach of any one of them demands equal punishment.

This is a matter that ought to be weighed seriously by those who break the Sabbath. Men who steal, who commit adultery, or who murder are equally as guilty as those who neglect their sabbatical duty <note>The religious obligation to rest and worship on the seventh day.</note>.

Because of this, we find these crimes so constantly joined together in the Bible when God’s judgments are announced. This is also why the command to keep the Sabbath is so often and so strongly insisted upon.

Furthermore, the Sabbath is frequently presented as the condition for national prosperity; it is the requirement for a flourishing state and for the stability of the royal family. Just as often, the neglect of it brings a denunciation of heavy anger, and the threats and judgments of almighty power.

It is this neglect that was the chief cause of the Jews being forced into the miserable bondage of the Babylonian captivity <term>Babylonish captivity: the period in the 6th century BCE when the Jewish people were exiled to Babylon after the fall of Jerusalem</term>.

*Nehemiah* <note>A biblical leader who oversaw the rebuilding of Jerusalem and enforced religious laws after the exile.</note>, being highly sensible of this, spoke out passionately against the offenders and punished them regarding this specific matter: *treading the wine-press on the sabbath, bringing in their corn, carrying all manner of burdens, and selling food, fish, and all kinds of goods in the market.*

*Then, he says, I confronted the nobles of Judah and said to them: "What evil thing is this that you are doing, profaning the sabbath?"*

*"Did your fathers not do the same? And did our GOD not bring all this evil upon us and on our city? Yet you bring even more wrath upon Israel by profaning the sabbath."*

These words are remarkable and should not be passed over without close attention.

--- Page 146 ---
<meta>Continuity: Page 103 concluded with Nehemiah confronting the nobles of Judah for allowing the Sabbath to be profaned through trade and labor. This page continues that argument, detailing the responsibilities of leaders and the prophetic promises associated with the Sabbath.</meta>

[ 104 ]

<meta>catchword: Nehemiah</meta>

The holy prophet <note>Nehemiah, a central figure in the rebuilding of Jerusalem after the exile.</note> argues with the nobles of Judah. Why? It was not necessarily that they themselves were treading the wine-press, reaping the grain, carrying heavy loads, or selling fish—the various charges listed in the indictment here. Rather, it was their duty to have prevented and punished such shameful offenses. They were expected to set a virtuous example by keeping the Sabbath themselves and ensuring that their subordinates did the same.

The nobles should not excuse themselves from public worship to go riding, traveling, or conducting any worldly business. As the guardians of the public and the face of the government, they possess the necessary **coercive powers** <term>coercive powers: the legal authority to use force or penalties to ensure laws are followed</term>. They must enforce the law against such offenders in the same way that *Nehemiah* later prescribed, which ultimately corrected the problem.

It is both the duty and in the best interest of the nobles—that is, of a good government—to seek the favor of GOD upon the nation and upon the royal family, which stands as a clear example of GOD’S blessing.

We find a striking record <note>original: "glaring document"</note> of this principle in *Jeremiah* XVII. 24: 

> *“It shall come to pass, says the Lord, if you diligently listen to me, and bring no heavy loads through the gates of this city on the sabbath day, but keep the sabbath day holy and do no work on it;*

> *Then there shall enter through the gates of this city kings and princes, sitting upon the throne of David, riding in chariots and on horses; they and their princes, the men of Judah, and the inhabitants of Jerusalem: and this city shall remain for ever.”*

After this promise follows the **denunciation** <note>A formal warning of divine punishment or condemnation.</note>. Because they failed to listen, they were eventually driven through those same gates as captives, treated like beasts of burden. Their city, their temple, their nation, and their entire system of government were destroyed.

<summary>This page discusses the specific responsibility of government leaders to enforce Sabbath laws and lead by example, linking national stability and the survival of the monarchy to religious obedience.</summary>

<meta>Continuity: This page continues from page 104, where the author argued that the prophet Nehemiah targeted the ruling class (the nobles) for the neglect of the Sabbath, as they were the primary architects of law and social order.</meta>

<sig>P</sig>

*Nehemiah* very pertinently applied his message to the *Nobles*, who are men of sense and learning, fit to govern, enact laws, and see them executed.

But chiefly, *Nobles* ought to set the *example*; it is the duty of their station. The majority of mankind does not see the fitness and reason of things; they are to be led to their duty. It is a tremendous consideration that if the *Nobles* become culpable, they are responsible not for their own faults alone, but for those of the age to come. It is dreadful to think if their punishment is to keep pace with the evil consequences!

It is not for nothing that God Almighty was pleased to give us his own *example* <note>Referring to the biblical account of God resting on the seventh day after Creation.</note> as a high sanction of this holy rest. Our blessed Savior, while on earth, was particularly careful in this regard. It was his constant custom to go to the synagogues on the Sabbath day, wherever he was. The Evangelists <note>The authors of the four Gospels: Matthew, Mark, Luke, and John.</note> testify to this in general. Many of his beneficent miracles were performed there, as were some of his discourses.

It is the greatest solecism <term>solecism: here used to mean a fundamental error or absurdity against the natural order</term> in nature to think that government can be supported without it, or that Society can subsist. They are so closely connected as to be one individual: the Sabbath is the soul, and matrimony <note>The institution of marriage, which the author views alongside the Sabbath as the two foundational pillars of human society.</note> is the body of the *common-weal* <term>common-weal: an archaic term for the "commonwealth" or the general welfare of the public</term>. Those who neglect the Sabbath have no right to the blessings of Society. They can claim no title to the public comforts of life—peace, plenty, and the like—since they have not joined in the common petitions for them at the hands of our bountiful Creator. They can hope for no private blessings on themselves and their families. They have no love

of their country: forfeit a claim to humanity itself; for a just definition of a man is, that he is a *religious* animal, as well as *social* <note>The author is adapting Aristotle’s famous definition of man as a "social animal" (*zoon politikon*), arguing that religious devotion is equally essential to human nature.</note>. The high faculties of his mind—which should lead him to his true glory—become the grounds for his condemnation if he lacks a due sense of religion, of the duty he owes to GOD, and to Society: specifically, the worship of the Deity and the *example* he owes to his fellow-creatures.

GOD worked six days. These he has given *us* for work. The seventh day he *rested* from work, to teach us not to work on the day which he has sanctified. He did not merely order or command it as a legal precept, but led us by his own path. Let us follow this glorious, this shining example. For as my learned friend Dr. *Middleton* <note>Conyers Middleton (1683–1750), an English clergyman and scholar whose "Life of Cicero" was a standard 18th-century text.</note>, in the *Life of Cicero*, thus writes with great justness on page 556:

> “This great man, *Cicero*, took the system of the world, 
> “or the visible works of God, to be the promulgation 
> “of God’s law; or the declaration of his will to man-
> “kind. Whence, as we might collect his Being, na-
> “ture, and attributes; so we could trace the reasons 
> “also, and motives of his acting; till by observing 
> “what *he* had done, we might know what *we* ought 
> “to do; and by the operations of the divine reason, 
> “be instructed how to perfect our own. Since the 
> “perfection of man consisted in the imitation of 
> “God.”

And thus we see how *Cicero* comments, as it were, upon *MOSES* <note>Moses is cited here as the traditional author of the Pentateuch, including the account of the Creation and the Ten Commandments.</note>, in regard to the Sabbath.

In conclusion, we must assert that the sabbatic observance only, both makes and preserves the impression

### VII.

# Sunday's MEDITATION.

->*June the 10th, 1759.*<-

# SACRED CRITICISM.

> Behold how all things rejoice in the age to come. <note>original: "Aspice venturo lætentur ut omnia sæclo." This quote is from Virgil’s Fourth Eclogue. In the 18th century, many scholars believed Virgil was unknowingly prophesying the birth of Christ, similar to the Hebrew prophets.</note>
->**VIRGIL.**<-

IT appears to me an undoubted truth, that the ancestors of mankind, and the patriarchs <term>patriarchs: the biblical fathers of the human race, such as Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob</term>, had very precise notions, of many particulars, relating to the expected *Messiah*. Such truths were communicated to them in prophetic notices from time to time; handed down through all ages, and dispersed among all people. They knew the nature of his birth—that it was a divine birth <term>geniture: a formal or archaic term for the process of being born or begotten</term>; that he was to be born of a Virgin, without any human communi- <note>The text ends mid-word; the catchword "cation" indicates the full word is "communication," likely referring to the doctrine of the Virgin Birth without human intervention.</note>

<summary>In this meditation from June 1759, the author argues that ancient patriarchs possessed specific, divinely inspired knowledge about the future Messiah. He cites Virgil’s poetry as evidence that these prophetic ideas were shared across different cultures and eras.</summary>
<keywords>Messiah, prophecy, Virgil, patriarchs, Sacred Criticism, divine birth, 18th-century theology</keywords>

--- Page 152 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page continues the argument from the previous page, detailing the specific prophetic knowledge the author believes ancient patriarchs and pagan cultures possessed regarding the life, death, and dual nature of the Messiah before his arrival.</meta>

cation of a man <note>This completes the word "communication" from the previous page, referring to the belief that the Messiah would be born of a Virgin without human intervention.</note>, but to be called the Son of God; that his mother's name was to be *Mary*.

They knew, that he was to reform the world, and that he was to die <note>original: "dye"</note>, for the demerits of all mankind; that he was to die by crucifixion, and even at the time of the spring equinox <note>original: "vernal equinox"</note>; as his birth was to be at the winter solstice <term>winter solstice: the shortest day of the year, midwinter, which the author links to the traditional date of Christmas</term>; and in a rocky cave; when the whole world was in peace.

They knew, that he was to arise to life again, and ascend to his father in heaven. In short, they knew, he was really a divine person, a person of the Deity; at the same time, had the human nature; was, as we may say, a *God-man* <term>God-man: a translation of the Greek term 'theanthropos,' describing Christ as being simultaneously fully God and fully human</term>.

All these particulars, and many more of the like, I could prove very largely, from human, as well as sacred testimonies. In time, among mankind, these notices degenerated into story, and allegory. All the eastern world, where they were first communicated, was ever fond, and is so still, of a pompous, figurative manner of speech, in narration, in writing; rhetorical flowers, *similes*, marvelous images, and expressions, symbol, allegory.

The Greeks did the like; but through extreme ingenuity, they turned them all into mere fable; altered, and corrupted names, and circumstances; yet so, as that with moderate sagacity, we can fish out the latent truth; and the original plan, on which they have built their pleasing stories. Moreover they drew everything, all antiquity, into their own country.

*Ovid*'s elegant book of *Metamorphoses* <note>original: "Metamorphofis"; a famous Latin narrative poem by Ovid depicting the history of the world through mythological transformations.</note> is an everlasting fund of materials, to prove my assertion. And

<summary>The author argues that ancient cultures worldwide held specific, accurate prophecies of Christ’s life—from his birth to Mary to his crucifixion at the equinox—but that these truths were eventually distorted into myths and fables by the Greeks and Romans.</summary>

--- Page 153 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page continues the argument that Greek mythology preserved ancient, sacred truths about the Messiah. The previous page ended with "so, as that with mode..." suggesting that with moderate effort, one can see the truth within the fables.</meta>

These poetical works have <note>This completes the thought from the previous page: "with moderate effort, these..."</note> preserved innumerable sacred records of the ancient world; though they are wrapped in a pleasing style of fable. The poet himself knew them to be truth at the core, handed down by word of mouth and in traditions by the Greeks before they became masters of historical writing.

I have many years ago printed a plain proof of *Jehovah* <note>The author identifies the God of the Old Testament with the figure of the Messiah/Son of God</note>, the divine hero of the Jews, their leader, patron, oracle, and their God; hidden under the pagan fable of *Bacchus* <note>Bacchus is the Roman name for the Greek god Dionysus; the author argues this pagan figure was a corrupted memory of the true God</note>.

It cannot be questioned but that the story of the birth of *Bacchus* springs from ancient notions of the birth of the true Son of God. His mother’s name, *Semele*, is pure Hebrew; the name of God, the "Name of God" <note>original: "Shem el," from the Hebrew 'Shem' (name) and 'El' (God)</note>. Being the daughter of *Cadmus* points out to us where the fable came from—*Phoenicia*—and the time of its transportation from Asia to Greece is not distant from the truth.

At the same time, it acquaints us with a piece of mere history that is both valuable and of high antiquity.

The sacred historian, in *Genesis chapter 10*, tells us the Hivite was the son of *Canaan*, son of *Ham*. This Hivite (using the family name *Hevæus* <note>The Latinized version of the biblical 'Hivite'</note>) was the same as the Cadmonite mentioned in *Genesis 15:19*, who is no other than our *Cadmus*. At least the *Cadmus* of the Greeks was a near descendant from that original stock.

Though his posterity, as well as the rest of the Canaanite nations, became idolaters, yet *he* followed the patriarchal religion; and he rendered himself particularly eminent for erecting a serpentine temple, or *Dracontium* <term>Dracontium: a massive megalithic temple (like a stone circle) arranged in the shape of a serpent, which the author believed were built by ancient patriarchs</term>, such as that immense work at Abury <note>The Great Stone Circle at Avebury in Wiltshire, England</note> in Wiltshire and Shap in Westmorland.

<summary>The author argues that the Greek myth of Bacchus is a corrupted version of the history of Jehovah, linking the figure Cadmus to the biblical Hivites and the construction of serpent-shaped stone temples in Britain.</summary>
<keywords>Bacchus, Jehovah, Semele, Cadmus, Phoenicia, Genesis, Hivites, Dracontium, Avebury, megalithic temples, biblical archaeology</keywords>

--- Page 154 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page continues the author's argument identifying the mythological Cadmus with the biblical Hivites (Cadmonites). The previous page established that the story of Bacchus and his mother Semele (whose name the author derives from the Hebrew for "Name of God") originated in Phoenicia and traveled to Greece.</meta>

These temples were formed of huge unhewn stones, laid out in the form of a snake, passing through a circle made of similar stones, all set upright in the earth. <note>The author is describing "dracontia" or serpent-temples, such as Avebury in England, which he believed were built by ancient patriarchs to represent religious truths.</note>

Hence, mythology created the story of *Cadmus* and his wife *Hermione* being turned into snakes; of *Cadmus* killing a snake and sowing its teeth, with men springing up from them, and other similar tales.

The name of *Hevæus* <note>The Latinized form of "Hivite"</note>, or Hivite—called in the scriptures a *Cadmonite*—comes from the word for *snake* in Hebrew <note>The Hebrew word "khivvah" is traditionally linked to "serpent" or "snake"</note>. *Cadmus* was the son of *Agenor*, who is at least a descendant of the **CANAAN** mentioned by *Moses*, the son of **HAM**. This descendant was called *Agenor* by the Greeks, and also identified as their deity *Mercury*, because he began the trade of a merchant and was a keeper of sheep. *Europa*, the daughter of *Agenor* (or *Cna* in the Phoenician language <note>The name "Cna" is an ancient form of "Canaan"</note>), was carried away to Crete by *Jupiter*, the king there, and gave her name to Europe. His ship bore the emblem of a white bull, which provided the basis for the famous fable. This *Jupiter* was one of the Curetes <note>Ancient attendants or guardians in Greek myth</note>, also known as the *Dioscuri* <note>The "Divine Twins," Castor and Pollux</note>, who were the first people to build ships after **NOAH**. They followed the patriarchal religion and were close relations of *Melchisedec* <note>A biblical priest-king who represents the pure, original religion before it was corrupted by paganism</note>. The historian *Sanchoniathon* <note>An ancient Phoenician author whose works survive only in fragments; he was often cited to prove the historical reality behind myths</note> explains these matters.

*Hermione*, the wife of *Cadmus*—or *Harmonia*, as some call her—gave her name to mount *Hermon*, so famous in sacred scripture. Upon mentioning this, I made the following critical observation:

> Psalm 133:3. *As the dew of Hermon, which fell upon the hill of Zion*.

This is a comparison or illustration of the sacred ointment poured upon *Aaron’s* head, which ran down his beard and then to the edges of his garments. <note>The author is connecting the physical geography of the Holy Land and its mythology back to the rituals of the biblical priesthood.</note>

<summary>The author identifies the mythical Cadmus with the biblical Hivites, arguing that "serpent temples" and Greek fables are actually distorted historical records of biblical patriarchs and Phoenician migrations.</summary>
<keywords>Cadmus, Hivites, Serpent Temples, Megaliths, Phoenician History, Agenor, Europa, Mount Hermon, Biblical Archaeology, Euhemerism</keywords>

--- Page 155 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page transitions from discussing the mythological origins of Cadmus and the Hivites (Cadmonites) to addressing a specific geographical and textual puzzle in the Bible: Psalm 133, which compares brotherly unity to the "dew of Hermon" falling on "Mount Sion."</meta>

In vain have the learned tried to reconcile this to any propriety in the comparison, because the hills of **Hermon** and **Sion** <note>Mount Hermon is located in the far north of Israel/Lebanon, while Mount Sion (Zion) is in Jerusalem, roughly 100 miles away. This distance makes the biblical description of dew traveling from one to the other geographically puzzling.</note> are so far distant. Hear the most learned **Reland** <note>Hadrian Reland (1676–1718) was a Dutch scholar and pioneering cartographer of the Holy Land.</note> for all the rest. *Palestine, Volume I, page 325* <note>original: "Palæst. Tom. I. p. 325."</note>:
“He professes himself far from being satisfied with what explanations the critics have made on this head. Nor can he satisfy himself, he frankly owns, so as to strike out any just meaning.”

He proceeds to give us his own opinion, which is as little to the purpose as the rest. And he quotes too our **Maundrell** <note>Henry Maundrell (1663–1701) wrote a famous 17th-century travelogue of the Levant, noted for its empirical observations.</note> to help him out, who says, “the dew is excessive great that falls upon mount **Hermon**, he being an eye witness.”

No doubt but the **Psalmist** <note>The author of the biblical Book of Psalms; this refers specifically to Psalm 133:3.</note> means to compare the copious effusion <note>A pouring out or overflowing.</note> of the ointment to the copious effusion, or descent of dew, upon the mountain. So far is good sense. But the difficulty is this: how can the dew that falls on mount **Hermon** be said to descend thence on mount **Sion**, as the ointment fell from **Aaron’s** head to his beard; and thence down to the skirts of the sacred and magnificent vestments?

These mountains of **Hermon** and **Sion** are many miles asunder and have no continuity or relation to one another. The difficulty of understanding the passage therefore lies,

1. In wrong notions of the mountain **Hermon**, which had its name from **Hermione**, the wife of **Cadmus**: in the country of the **Hivites** (or **Cadmonites**). <note>The author is attempting to solve the geographical problem by arguing that "Hermon" and "Sion" might refer to something other than the distant mountains, or that their names are linked through the ancient Hivite/Cadmonite culture he discussed on the previous page.</note>
2. In a false reading of the place.

<summary>The author critiques various scholars' failed attempts to explain the geographical impossibility of dew from Mount Hermon falling on Mount Sion in Psalm 133. He suggests the confusion stems from a misunderstanding of the name Hermon and its connection to the mythological Hermione and the Hivites.</summary>

<keywords>Psalm 133, Mount Hermon, Mount Sion, Hadrian Reland, Henry Maundrell, Aaron's anointing, Hivites, Cadmonites, biblical geography, Hermione</keywords>

--- Page 156 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page continues the argument regarding the geographical confusion in Psalm 133. The previous page established that scholars like Reland and Maundrell struggled to explain how dew from Mount Hermon in the north could fall on Mount Sion (Zion) in Jerusalem, over 100 miles away.</meta>

Regarding the first point: Mount *Hermon* is commonly, but mistakenly, thought to be the one located on the east side of the Plain of *Esdraelon* <note>The Jezreel Valley in northern Israel.</note>, near Mount *Gilboa* and below Mount *Tabor*. However, the true *Hermon* intended is the one located much further north, at the source of the River *Jordan*; it forms the northern border of the land of *Palestine* and is a part of the great *Lebanon* <note>original: "Libanus"</note> mountain range.

As for the first *Hermon* mentioned, even that is far enough from Mount *Sion*; it has no connection at all to Mount *Sion*. But when we recognize the latter mountain as the true Hermon, the difficulty in the Psalm disappears.

2. We correct the reading of the word *Sion* to *Sirion* <note>Sirion is the ancient Phoenician/Sidonian name for Mount Hermon, as noted in Deuteronomy 3:9.</note>. The passage should read: "like the dew of *Hermon*, which fell upon the hill of *Sirion*." Our standard translation is also wrong in its sense, as if the dew fell only once; it is also wrong in number, as if there were only one mountain of *Sion*. According to the original Hebrew, the version ought to be:

> “Like the dew of *Hermon* falling upon the mountains of *Sirion*.”

The word *mountains* in the plural is enough to assure us that this cannot be the more famous Mount *Sion* at *Jerusalem* being discussed here. That *Sion* is but one small hill, shaped like a castle, on which *David* built his palace, his city, his tomb, and the holy Tabernacle for the Ark. It remained so until *Solomon* built the Temple on a neighboring hill; afterward, that Temple hill was frequently, by a figure of speech, called Mount *Sion*. However, that location is never referred to in the plural as "mountains."

For this reason, the Syriac version, the Latin Vulgate <note>original: "vulgar Latin"; the standard 4th-century Latin translation of the Bible.</note>, and the Arabic version in our primary text all deviate from the original Hebrew.

<meta>catchword: ginal.</meta>

<summary>The author resolves a biblical geographical puzzle by arguing that Psalm 133 refers to Mount Sirion (a peak near Hermon) rather than Mount Sion in Jerusalem. This correction aligns the text with the physical reality of northern mountain dew cycles rather than suggesting a miraculous 100-mile journey for the mist.</summary>

<meta>Continuity: This page continues the argument that the "Sion" mentioned in Psalm 133 is actually "Sirion" (Mount Hermon). The previous page ended by discussing how the Hebrew text uses the plural "mountains," which does not fit the singular hill of Zion in Jerusalem.</meta>

original. Not understanding the true meaning <note>original: "purport"</note>, they translate it in the singular, *mountain*, and the Psalms in our Liturgy <note>The version found in the Book of Common Prayer.</note> do the same. In the Bible translation <note>The King James Version.</note>, it is correctly rendered as *mountains*, though the name *Sion* is still incorrect.

The translators of our Bibles, being aware of the geographical difficulty we are discussing, translate the passage this way: *as the dew of Hermon, and as the dew that descended upon the mountains of Zion.* In this rendering, the beauty of the comparison is entirely lost, because there is no physical continuity to make the metaphor work. For the comparison to be valid, the dew must flow from one hill to another, just as the holy oil flowed down from *Aaron’s* head, to his beard, and onto his clothing. These translators correctly used the plural *mountains*, yet that term is not at all appropriate for the single hill of *Sion* at *Jerusalem.*

But *Hermon* and *Sirion* <note>Spelled "Sirijon" in the original text.</note> are adjacent mountains, with one positioned just below the other.

*Deuteronomy 3:9. Hermon*, the Sidonians <note>The people of Sidon, a Phoenician city-state.</note> call *Sirion*; but the Amorites call it *Shenir.*

*Song of Solomon 4:8. Shenir* and *Hermon.* The Song of Solomon may be thought of as a series of duets between a male and female speaker. Here, the king speaks:

> Lebanon, my spouse, with me from Lebanon: look 
> from the top of Amana, from the top of Shenir and 
> Hermon: from the lions' dens, from the mountains of 
> the leopards.”

This is part of a song composed by the king when he was entertaining his Egyptian wife <note>Pharaoh's daughter.</note> at his tower or summer palace on Mount *Lebanon*, which looked toward *Damascus*—the most beautiful view in the world.

And this among the Greeks was the most common origin of idolatry and mythology. Let us now return to *Cadmus* after this digression—appropriate for a Sunday's reflection <note>original: "meditation"</note>. It should be understood that some later hero of *Cadmus*'s family is intended here, whose actions have been merged <note>original: "consolidated"</note> with the original figure from earlier <note>original: "higher"</note> times. He consults the oracle on where he should establish his home <note>original: "seat"</note>; he was ordered not to return unless he found his sister *Europa*, who had been carried away from *Phoenicia* by a young king of *Crete* called *Jupiter* <note>In Greek myth, Zeus took the form of a bull to abduct the Phoenician princess Europa.</note>. He, as I previously mentioned, was one of the *Dioscouri* <term>Dioscouri: Usually refers to the "Heavenly Twins" Castor and Pollux, but the author here identifies them as some of the earliest pioneers of seafaring and trade.</term>, the first traders and navigators. <margin>Genesis 25:22</margin>
The practice of consulting oracles originated in *Canaan*, at *Salem* <note>The ancient name for Jerusalem.</note>, where *Melchizedek* presided. It was there that *Rebekah* went to consult God (Genesis 25:22). The ox that *Cadmus* encounters and follows represents a sacrifice that he prepared to offer for the success of his new project: the founding of a city. He sends his men to a fountain, as water is always necessary for such rituals. The snake that kills his men—and which he later kills—signifies the *Dracontium* <term>Dracontium: A "serpent temple" made of standing stones arranged in a winding, snake-like path, such as those the author identified at Avebury in Wiltshire.</term>, or serpentine temple. He built this of stones set into the ground in a winding shape, just like those we have in *Britain*.

> “He killed the snake by throwing a massive stone—one with
> “such force that the strongest walls would have been

We should observe that the Greeks recount all these most ancient stories, concerning the people of the patriarchal religion <note>The original, "pure" religion of the Biblical patriarchs before it was corrupted into paganism.</note>, in the style and manner of their own times—having lapsed into idolatry—even though the persons they spoke of were not idolaters themselves.

In later ages, when the worship of the Hebrew *Jehovah* under the name of *Bacchus* <note>The author is using a common 18th-century theory that pagan gods were actually distorted versions of Biblical figures or concepts.</note> became famous and was brought over to Thebes, they grafted this worship into the house of their founder. *Semele*, a daughter of *Cadmus*, is said to have become pregnant by *Jupiter*, who appeared in his divine majesty, the splendor of which consumed her. The child, the young *Bacchus*, is snatched from the flames and sewn up in his father’s thigh, where he remains until the time of his birth.

Thus, he is "twice born," by both human and divine parentage. He possesses two natures: God and man, as is often stated in the hymns of *Orpheus* <note>Orpheus was a legendary Greek hero and musician; the "Orphic Hymns" are a collection of ancient poems dedicated to various gods.</note>. The *Hyacinthian* festival was of an immoderate lamentation, first had by the women for his death; then of as great a rejoicing for his coming to life again. The whole derived from notions among the ancestors of mankind concerning the death and resurrection of the *Messiah*. Of this I have spoke already.

Another famous story in fabulous antiquity <note>That is, the antiquity described in myths and fables.</note> is that of *Myrrha*, who became with child by her father; which child was *Adonis*, whose death was the origin of another most famous Greek festival, *Adonia*, celebrated with like lamentations and rejoicings. Which evidently express the knowledge they had of a divine hero that was to be born and die for mankind, and be raised to life again. *Myrrha* is Mary. *Adonis* is Lord in Hebrew <note>The author is linking the name Adonis to the Hebrew word *Adon* or *Adonai*, meaning "Lord."</note>.

### VIII.

## Sunday's MEDITATION.

# H E S C O L. <note>The title "Hescol" likely refers to the biblical "Eshcol" (Hebrew for "cluster"), a valley known for its fruitful vines. The author often uses such names to signify a collection of gathered wisdom or "fruits" of research.</note>

## BRITISH ORIGINS. <note>original: "ORIGINES BRITTANICÆ."</note>

> The course of studies should not begin with Theology itself: but the mind must first be exercised in other arts and disciplines. <note>original: "Non eſſe curſum ſtudiorum, ab ipſa Theologiâ incipiendum: ſed exercendum prius animum in aliis artibus, & diſciplinis."</note>

<meta>Continuity: On the previous page, the author began explaining his theory that the original religion of Britain was not pagan, but a pure form of "patriarchal" worship. He concluded by stating that his research into this ancient faith revealed it to...</meta>

...be the same as Christianity. Of course, I must be drawn into an inquiry concerning the original settling <note>original: "planting" — a 17th and 18th-century term for colonization or the establishing of a new population.</note> of Britain. I found that they came here by sea in the earliest times of the world, before the opposite continent was well peopled, and before idolatry began.

The Druids are so universally famous for their worship in groves that they naturally lead us to the first Arch-druid, ABRAHAM. In pursuing these inquiries, I perceived how they and their people were connected with the illustrious Patriarch; and, in the main, were his descendants from the Arabian branch, through his last wife, Keturah. <note>In the Book of Genesis, Keturah is Abraham's second wife. The author is following a historical theory—popular in his time—that Keturah's children migrated to the West, eventually becoming the ancestors of the Britons.</note>

Upon making a critical examination of her history, I discerned that the learned commentators on the sacred scriptures, both Jewish and Christian, have not correctly understood it, due to a false fear of not keeping strictly to the narrative provided by Moses.

Consequently, this work requires me to consider the chronology of ABRAHAM as the framework upon which to conduct it. This must be done with more attention than has previously been given. In doing so, we must seek greater clarity in the history of the Patriarch, which will, at the same time, greatly assist us in identifying the ancient history of Britain.

We shall find, from sufficient evidence, that they are connected together. Therefore, we shall not wonder that the form of religion practiced by our good Patriarch was the same as that brought here by the Druids. We will see how mistaken all our writers are who imagine the Druids to be no better than other pagan <note>original: "heathen"</note> priests.

We should not be surprised by the countless Druid Temples spread across the British Isles, or the vast quantities of those bronze instruments called Celts <term>Celt: An archaeological term for a prehistoric bronze or stone axe head; the author believes these were used by Druids in religious rituals</term>, which belonged to their religious ceremonies in the most ancient times.

If we examine the sacred history of ABRAHAM, we can see that *Moses* continues the narrative in a steady flow until the marriage of his son *Isaac*, which occurs at the end of the twenty-fourth chapter of *Genesis*. This is essentially the sacred history of the Jewish people; for they—and eventually the *Messiah* <term>Messiah: The promised savior or 'Anointed One'</term>—were to descend from *Isaac*. This was the primary focus of *Moses*'s writing.

After completing that, it was appropriate for him to finish the history of ABRAHAM to make it complete. This he does in the following ten verses of chapter twenty-five, concluding with Abraham's other offspring from his last marriage to *Keturah*, as well as his death, burial, and his last will and testament: describing how he distributed the great wealth with which God had blessed him.

He begins the chapter:
> *Then Abraham took another wife, and her name was Keturah.*
> *And she bore him Zimran, Jokshan, Medan, Midian, Ishbak, and Shuah.*

He proceeds by listing their descendants and noting that Abraham gave his inheritance to *Isaac*. However, to the sons of the concubines whom *Abraham* had, he gave gifts and sent them away from his son *Isaac* while he was still alive, into the "east country"—that is, Arabia. It is very clear from this that ABRAHAM was not in a state of frail old age when he married *Keturah* as a secondary wife. He died at the age of one hundred and seventy-five, which is one hundred

The sons of *Keturah* are not so much as mentioned as assisting at the great funeral. They had gone off long ago into their destined inheritance in *Arabia*; they were married and had families of their own—some of their sons and grandsons were just mentioned by *Moses*.

Now it is evident, if we consider the matter attentively, that this whole affair of his marrying *Keturah*, and his children and grandchildren by her, is recited only as a kind of supplement—an appendix to the history of this great progenitor <note>progenitor: a direct ancestor or forefather</note> of the people of the *Jews*. It begins, *Then again Abraham took a wife*—it is not intended to be inserted in chronological order. It is recommencing a new period; though not to be omitted, it was not necessary to be inserted in the order of time.

The *Rabbins* <term>Rabbins: An older English term for Rabbis, the Jewish scholars and teachers of religious law.</term> and the *Christian* commentators must be greatly mistaken to think this happened after *Abraham* was old and "well stricken in years," as mentioned at the beginning of the preceding chapter; or after Queen *Sarah*, as *Josephus* <term>Josephus: Titus Flavius Josephus, a first-century Romano-Jewish historian whose writings provide historical context for the Biblical period.</term> calls her, was dead—at the time when he was providing a wife for his son *Isaac*, the great heir of the family and of the promise (that is, of the *Messiah*).

They must certainly be mistaken against nature and all degrees of probability to think *Abraham* married in a decrepit old age, after *Isaac* was married and after *Sarah* was dead, and that he had so many children then. It is a false tenderness toward his character to consider it blameworthy for him to take a wife, or rather a concubine, while *Sarah* lived. <note>The author argues that Abraham’s marriage to Keturah likely happened while Sarah was still alive, rather than in his extreme old age, suggesting the Biblical narrative is grouped by subject rather than strict timeline.</note>
