lived. *Keturah* is expressly called *concubine* <note>concubine: In this historical and biblical context, a woman of lower social status than a primary wife, though her children were still recognized as part of the family lineage.</note>, in *1 Chronicles 1:32*, as also in the chapter before us, *Genesis 25:6*.

It never was reckoned criminal for a man to take a concubine in the eastern part of the world, even to this day. This union with *Keturah* seems particularly to have been done by the will of heaven at a time when the world needed peopling and when the true religion needed to be spread abroad; God purposed that the major part of the globe should be peopled by the posterity of *Abraham*, as it is this day.

The major part of *Asia* and the major part of *Africa* are peopled now by *Abraham's* Arabian descendants, as is most part of the *Turkish* empire <note>Turkish empire: Referring to the Ottoman Empire, which controlled much of the Middle East, North Africa, and Southeast Europe at the time this was written.</note>. The *Jews* abound in all quarters of the globe, including great numbers in *Europe*. I take all our aboriginal *Britons* to have been of the Arabian stem, and so are their remains at this day: the *Cornish*, *Irish*, *Manks* <note>original: "Manks"; referring to the Manx people of the Isle of Man.</note>, *Ersk* <note>original: "Ersk"; an archaic term for Erse, referring to the Gaelic-speaking people of Scotland or Ireland.</note>, *Highland Scots*, and the *Welsh* in some degree, though later than the others. This seems to have been the purpose of heaven in this match with *Keturah*.

Let us review the history. It is certain from the express words of *Genesis 25:6* that *Abraham* sent these sons of *Keturah* into Arabia with suitable gifts to put them into a proper way of livelihood. He sent them away at a distance because they were to have no part of the land of Canaan from *Isaac* his son during *his lifetime*. He thus sought to provide against the inconveniences of their proximity, which might occasion quarrels. It is reasonable to suppose they were then at man's estate <note>man's estate: adulthood.</note> and able to provide for themselves; and it is just as reasonable to suppose this was about the time

We must conclude then, that he took *Keturah* not long after the birth of *Isaac*. The birth of *Isaac* was purely supernatural. *Sarah* well knew she should have no more children. She therefore gave *Keturah* to her husband, as she had formerly given him *Hagar*. He had seventy years of his life remaining. No wonder then he fathered six sons, who were fit to be sent away by the time *Isaac* married.

Concubinage <note>concubinage: A legal status in antiquity where a man had a secondary wife; in this context, the author views it as a divinely permitted method for rapid population growth.</note> was no crime, and when the world needed people, it was necessary. So *Rachel*, when she found herself barren, gave her maid to her husband. And *Leah*, when she found she had stopped having children, gave her maid to her husband.

Though the time of ABRAHAM taking *Keturah* cannot be precisely known, we may make a reasonable approximation of it; which is sufficient for our purpose, based on the connection between the history of the holy Patriarch and the Egyptian and Phoenician *Hercules*, who is the HESCOL of *Moses* <note>Hescol: Referring to Eshcol (Genesis 14), whom the author identifies here with the mythical Hercules.</note>, the ally of ABRAHAM, who brought the first colony to Britain.

*Herodotus* in *Clio* <note>original: "Clio"; the first book of Herodotus's *Histories*.</note> writes that, among the customs of the Persians, a son is not admitted to the presence of his father, but is brought up by the women until he attains the age of five years. And this custom ABRAHAM brought with him from the East.

There is another circumstance in the sacred history
which very much confirms my stating this point of

Though ABRAHAM left Mamre by Hebron, re-
moving southward to Beersheba, and there *Isaac* was
born, yet it appears that he kept his farm at Mamre,
and *Sarah* lived chiefly there with *Isaac* as he grew up to

happened while ABRAHAM dwelt at Beersheba with
*Keturah*. So it seems to have been a wise arrange-
ment to prevent that inconvenience and the uneasi-

lived with her—and to prevent likewise his children by *Ke-
turah* from quarreling with *Isaac*’s children.

*Isaac* brought his wife into his mother’s tent and
lived there, at Mamre, while his father lived with his
new family at Beersheba. This gives us a truer no-
tion of this part of the history of the great Patriarch’s
life than is common, especially regarding the latter half of it.

| 1830 | The *Cabiri Dioscuri* <note>Cabiri Dioscuri: Mystery deities and "sons of Zeus" in Greek myth, here presented as historical figures who were early pioneers of seafaring and metallurgy.</note>, sons of *Sydic*, dwell at Berytus, build ships; navigate to Crete, and Cyprus, for iron, and copper: build a patriarchal temple on mount *Caffius*. |

All these and the like particulars, I shall endeavour, sufficiently to prove, from united testimonies of sacred, and profane <note>In this context, "profane" refers to secular or non-religious historical records from Greek and Roman sources.</note> history.

I have only to add at present, this consideration: that the part of the Amorites <note>A powerful ancient people in Canaan; the author identifies them as the ancestors of the Gibeonites.</note>, which were the descendants of our **Hescol**, and his brothers **Aner** and **Mamre** were those Gibeonites in **David**’s time; who were not lapsed into the common idolatry; but preserved likewise that punctuality of their ancient faith, in the sacrifice of the vernal equinox <note>The spring equinox, occurring around March 21st.</note>: the same custom as remained with our Druids <note>The author seeks to prove that the British Druids were not pagans, but followers of a "patriarchal" religion brought from the Levant.</note> (who came from them) of sacrificing then, with human blood; from notions of the great sacrifice then to be made, in the person of Messiah, at that time of the year.

They did it commonly at a revolution of five years <note>A reference to the "lustrum," a five-year period often cited in descriptions of ancient Gaulish and British sacrificial cycles.</note>, or as an extraordinary propitiatory <note>An offering intended to appease or regain the favor of God.</note>, on any very public and grievous calamity: and with the blood of prisoners, and such as were otherwise condemned to die. And those victims so destined, accounted themselves particularly happy, in their reversionary state <note>A reference to the Druidic belief in the immortality of the soul or reincarnation, which the author suggests made the victims willing to die.</note>; who thus suffered.

These Gibeonites, like the house of the Kenites <note>A nomadic tribe related to Moses’s father-in-law.</note>, the Rechabites, lived under the umbrage <note>In this sense, "umbrage" means protection or shadow.</note>, and protection of the Israelites. When **Saul**, had contrary to public faith, ill-treated them, God sent a judgment upon the Israelites. And seven persons of his house were now doomed to suffer, for an atonement. **II. Sam. XXI.** <note>Reference to the biblical book of 2 Samuel, chapter 21, which recounts the Gibeonites demanding the execution of Saul's descendants to end a famine.</note>

The Gibeonites chose the time of the vernal equinox, for this demand, and they hanged them in the hill

I understand that they nailed them, once dead, to trees in the posture of crucifixion. Such was the method of our Druids <note>The author frequently attempts to synchronize Druidic practices with Biblical events to argue for a shared origin.</note>. "Hanging on a tree" in Scriptural language signifies crucifixion. In this way, the Philistines nailed the bodies of *Saul* and *Jonathan* in the form of crucifixion onto the wall of *Bethshan*, where the Philistines had hanged them, as expressed in verse 22 of this chapter.

In the New Testament, a similar expression is used regarding our Savior’s crucifixion: *Luke* 22:39 <note>The author likely refers to Luke 23:39, describing the "malefactors" or criminals.</note>, one of the criminals who were hanged—that is, crucified. *Acts* 5:30 says, "*killed and hanged on a tree*"; and *Acts* 10:39 uses the same phrasing. We conclude that these sons of *Saul* were not simply hanged in the modern sense; for had they been, they would have been taken down by evening, according to the usual custom <note>Jewish law in Deuteronomy 21:23 required that a body hanged on a tree be buried the same day.</note>. However, *Rizpah*, the concubine of *Saul*, watched over them day and night to protect them from birds of prey.

The time of this execution is specified in verse 9 as being at the start of the barley harvest, which corresponds to the vernal equinox <note>The spring equinox, occurring around March 21st.</note>. Our Britons, in part, descended from these people in the earliest ages.

I have often observed that remarkable truths are found in secular history <note>original: "profane history"</note> that illustrate sacred scripture. I will provide an instance now. *Macrobius* <note>A 5th-century Roman writer whose works were used by antiquarians to find clues about ancient religions.</note> states that *Hercules* used the <term>hebdomal character</term> <note>From the Greek 'hebdomas,' referring to a cycle of seven; here it means the seven-day week.</note> or the week. This Hercules is actually **Hescol** <note>The author identifies the mythological Hercules with the biblical Eshcol, an ally of Abraham mentioned in Genesis 14.</note>. This is an explicit testimony that **Abraham** also used the seven-day week. Furthermore:

The Mexicans began their year in the spring—a striking testimony that this was the original, primeval custom.

— *For which reason, the <term>mummys</term> <note>Mummies.</note> of the Egyptians; taught by the patriarch JOSEPH. 30.*
*St. Paul's metaphysical account of the resurrection, deduc'd from plants. 31.*
*No <term>equivocal generation</term> <note>The now-disproven theory that living organisms can spontaneously emerge from non-living matter, such as maggots from rotting meat.</note>. 32.*
*The immensity of creation. 43.*
— *The origin of springs and fountains. 45.*

- *The mistletoe of Christmas tide. 9.*

- *Architecture Arabian, oldest. 18.*

*Botanists lovers of nature. 24.*
- *Hence plants named from the fair sex. 25.* <note>The "fair sex" refers to women; many plants were historically named after goddesses, queens, or female virtues.</note>
*The wisdom of plants. 27.*

*Going to benediction, going to church. 79.*
*Court-levee on Sabbath day, pernicious. 84. 95. 97.* <note><term>Court-levee</term>: A formal morning reception or assembly held by a monarch or high-ranking noble. The author suggests that holding such social events on the Sabbath is harmful or wicked.</note>

## I N D E X.

*Sabbath the foundation of government. 89. 100.*
*Sabbath a natural, moral, religious duty. 91.*
*God’s example therein. 92. 104. 105.* <note>Referring to the divine rest on the seventh day of Creation.</note>
*’Tis Sabbath that makes the good impression on the heart. 99. 101. 102.*
*Nehemiah contends with the Nobles, for breach of the Sabbath. 104. 105.* <note>A reference to the biblical Book of Nehemiah, where the leader rebukes the ruling class for desecrating the holy day with commerce.</note>
*The prosperity of the nation, and royal family, due to its observation. 104.*
*Cicero’s notion of God’s example. 106.* <note>Marcus Tullius Cicero, the Roman philosopher; the author likely references his views on how human laws should mirror divine patterns.</note>
*Abraham observed the Sabbath. 133.*

## D R U I D S.

+ *ABRAHAM first Druid, planted religious groves. 8. 123.* <note>The author follows an 18th-century antiquarian theory that the biblical patriarchs and the British Druids shared the same ancient religion.</note>
*Our Druids from him, were of the patriarchal religion. 9. 16. 121.*
+ *Christmas, the Yule tide festival of the Druids. the birth of Messiah. 10.* <note>A claim that ancient midwinter celebrations were early recognitions of the coming of Christ.</note>
—+ *Mistletoe a type of Messiah. of Christianity. 11. of resurrection. 33.* + <note>The word "type" here means a symbolic foreshadowing.</note>
*They crossed their hands, on gathering it. 10.*
*Christ born at the winter solstice, a scripture proof of it. 12.*
—+ *A magical notion of the Druids. 15.*
— *Druids had knowledge of the Trinity. 17. 18.* <note>The author suggests the Druids understood the Christian concept of the Triune God before the arrival of the Gospel.</note>
— *The Druids had a <term>kebla</term> in their temples, of stone. 16.* <note>original: "kebla"</note> <term>kebla</term> <note>The "Qibla" is the direction of prayer in Islam; here, the author uses the term to describe the sacred focal point or orientation of Druidic stone circles.</note>
— *The Druid <term>kebla</term> on an oak. 17.*
*Isaiah mentions the mistletoe. 12.* <note>Likely interpreting a specific botanical reference in the Book of Isaiah as the sacred mistletoe.</note>
*Its berry like to an animal egg. 33.*
— *Balaam a Druid. 52. 75. 80.* <note>Balaam was a non-Israelite prophet in the Bible; here he is categorized as a member of the Druidic order.</note>
+ *Chyndonax Druid. 54.* <note>A name associated with a famous Druidic tomb discovered in Dijon, France, in 1598.</note>
— *Durbaleis Druid. 53.*
*Veleda, a royal Druidess of Germany. i.* <note>A historical prophetess of the Bructeri tribe who played a major role in the Batavian rebellion against Rome.</note>
*The Druids, and the Germans began their years six days after full moon. 66.*
— *Druid temples and groves, from ABRAHAM. 67.*
*Druids set the night before day. 71.* <note>A reference to the Celtic practice of measuring time by nights rather than days.</note>
—+ *The Druid May-day fires, in memory of creation. 72.* + <note>A reference to the festival of Beltane.</note>
— *The Druids wore the Phrygian bonnet. staff, belt, mantle. 15. 78.* —+ <note>A Phrygian bonnet is a soft, conical cap; the author is describing the presumed ritual costume of a Druid.</note>
*Human sacrifices from the earliest times. 133.*

<meta>This page continues the alphabetical index, transitioning from the previous page's focus on the Sabbath and Druidic traditions to the origins of Britain and various chronological milestones. The entries reflect the author's attempt to reconcile biblical history with Greek mythology and British national identity.</meta>

# I N D E X.

*Oration of praise <note>original: "Elogium"</note> on the founder, pages 2, 21, 47.*
*Solomon, the first botanist. 4, 7.* <note>The biblical King Solomon was traditionally credited with immense wisdom regarding the natural world, "from the cedar tree that is in Lebanon even unto the hyssop that springeth out of the wall."</note>
*God the first gardener. 22.*
*Solomon's seal. 9.* <note>Referring to the plant *Polygonatum*, which has root scars resembling a seal, though also alluding to the mystical signet ring of the King.</note>
*Dr. Stephen Hales. 32.* <note>An 18th-century clergyman and scientist famous for his pioneering studies on plant physiology and the measurement of blood pressure.</note>

---

# B R I T T A I N.

<margin>Eshcol—</margin>*Hescol, Hercules, a companion of Abraham, first peopled Britain. 126, 130.* <note>The author uses "Hescol" for the biblical Eshcol. This reflects a popular 18th-century theory that ancient British ancestors were connected to biblical patriarchs and that "Hercules" was actually a historical figure from the Middle East.</note>
*Apher, grandson of Abraham, his companion. 130.* <note>Apher is a biblical figure often associated in antiquarian texts with the naming of Africa or the migration of peoples to the West.</note>

---

# CHRONOLOGY.

*The Flood began and ended in May. 73.* <note>The author provides a specific seasonal date for the biblical Deluge.</note>
*The time of Cadmus. 111.* <note>Cadmus was the legendary Phoenician prince who founded Thebes and introduced the alphabet to the Greeks.</note>
*When the worship of Bacchus began, and from where. 116, 118.* <note>Bacchus is the Roman name for Dionysus, the god of wine and ecstasy.</note>
*When Abraham married Keturah, stated. 123.* <note>Keturah was the woman Abraham married after the death of Sarah.</note>
*A scheme of sacred chronology. 128.*
*Representation <note>original: "Type"</note> of the Creation week. 72.*
